
===PDF PAGE 1===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
IAN ZEMKE
Abstract. We construct cobordism maps on link Floer homology associated to decorated link
cobordisms.
The maps are deﬁned on a curved chain homotopy type invariant.
We describe
the construction, and prove invariance. We also make a comparison with the graph TQFT for
Heegaard Floer homology.

1. Introduction
Ozsv´ath and Szab´o constructed a powerful set of algebraic invariants of 3- and 4-manifolds, called
Heegaard Floer homology [OS04b] [OS06].
Heegaard Floer homology ﬁts into the framework of
a (3 + 1)-dimensional TQFT. Also deﬁned by Ozsv´ath and Szab´o [OS04a] and independently by
Rasmussen [Ras03], there is a reﬁnement of Heegaard Floer homology for knots embedded in 3-
manifolds, called knot Floer homology. There is a generalization of the knot invariant to links, due
to Ozsv´ath and Szab´o [OS08], called link Floer homology.
To a 4-manifold W with properly embedded surface Σ ⊆W, the boundary ∂Σ is a link in ∂W.
In light of the TQFT structure of the Heegaard Floer 3- and 4-manifold invariants, it is natural to
expect functorial maps between the link Floer homologies of the ends of a link cobordism (W, Σ).
An important step in this direction is the work of Juh´asz [Juh16], who constructed cobordism maps
for decorated link cobordisms on the hat version of link Floer homology. One limitation of Juh´asz’s
construction is that it uses the contact gluing map due to Honda, Kazez and Mati´c [HKM08], which
limits the construction to [
HFL, and not the full link Floer complex.
In this paper we provide a construction of a (3 + 1)-dimensional TQFT for a general version of
the link Floer complex, and prove invariance of the construction. The link invariant we consider in
this paper is a curved variation of the link Floer complexes described by Ozsv´ath and Szab´o [OS08].
This research was supported by NSF grant DMS-1703685.

arXiv:1610.05207v3  [math.GT]  2 Oct 2018

Given a ﬁnite set P = {p1, . . . , pn}, we deﬁne
R−
P := F2[Xp1, . . . , Xpn],
the polynomial ring freely generated by the variables Xp1, . . . , Xpn.
We will consider links L embedded in a 3-manifold, equipped with two disjoint collections of
basepoints, w and z, which are contained in L. We will write L = (L, w, z) for a link together with
two collections of basepoints. We will write Lσ for the multi-based link L, equipped with a map
σ: w ∪z →P .
We will construct a free and ﬁnitely generated R−
P -module
CFL−(Y, Lσ, s),
which also depends on a choice of Spinc structure s on Y . The module CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) has a natural
ﬁltration by ZP . We call the map σ a coloring of the link L.
We note also that the construction of CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) requires some auxiliary choices (a Heegaard
diagram and a choice of almost complex structure), so a precise deﬁnition of the invariant requires
some additional formalism. See Section 3 for a precise deﬁnition.
The module CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) comes with a distinguished endomorphism ∂, obtained by counting
pseudo-holomorphic curves, which satisﬁes
∂2 = ωL,σ · id,
for a scalar ωL,σ ∈R−
P .
Modules with an endomorphism squaring to the action of a scalar in the ground ring are often
referred to as matrix factorizations, and appear in the context of Khovanov-Rozansky homology
[KR08]. We will call a module with such an endomorphism a curved chain complex.
In this paper, a decorated link cobordism
(W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2)
between two multi-based links consists of a pair (W, Fσ) where W is a compact 4-manifold with
∂W = −Y1 ⊔Y2, and Fσ is a colored, oriented surface with divides. More explicitly, Fσ consists of a
properly embedded, oriented surface Σ in W, with a properly embedded collection of dividing arcs
A ⊆Σ, as well as a map σ: C(Σ\A) →P , where C(Σ\A) denotes the set of connected components
of Σ \ A. We further assume that
(1) ∂Σ = −L1 ⊔L2.
(2) A divides Σ into two subsurfaces, Σw and Σz, which meet along A. Furthermore Σw contains
the w-basepoints of L1 and L2, and Σz contains the z-basepoints.
A pictorial description of a decorated link cobordism is shown in Figure 1.1.
(Y1, L1)
(W, Fσ)
(Y2, L2)
Figure 1.1. A decorated link cobordism (W, Fσ). The solid dots denote w-
basepoints and the open dots denote z-basepoints. The shaded region is Σw, and
the unshaded region is Σz.

1.1. Invariants of decorated link cobordisms. Given a decorated link cobordism
(W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2)
and a Spinc structure s ∈Spinc(W), our paper centers on constructing a diﬀeomorphism invariant
of (W, Fσ), which takes the form of a cobordism map
FW,Fσ,s : CFL−(Y1, Lσ1
1 , s1) →CFL−(Y2, Lσ2
2 , s2),
where si = s|Yi and σi = σ|Li.
To construct the link cobordism maps, we describe maps on the link Floer complexes which
correspond to the following types of elementary cobordisms:
(1) 4-dimensional handle cobordisms for handles attached to the complement of the link.
(2) Cylindrical link cobordisms with simple dividing sets.
(3) Saddle cobordisms for band surgery on a link in a 3-manifold.
We deﬁne the cobordism maps for a general link cobordism (W, Fσ) by decomposing (W, Fσ) into a
sequence of such elementary cobordisms, and taking the composition of the maps for each elementary
piece.
Our paper centers on proving two main properties of the cobordism maps: diﬀeomorphism invari-
ance and functoriality.
Proving diﬀeomorphism invariance of the cobordism maps amounts to showing that the cobordism
maps are independent of the decomposition of (W, F) into elementary link cobordisms. To this end,
we state the following theorem:
Theorem A. If (W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is a decorated link cobordism, equipped with a Spinc
structure s ∈Spinc(W), then the map FW,Fσ,s constructed in this paper is a ZP -ﬁltered, R−
P -
equivariant chain map, which is a diﬀeomorphism invariant, up to ZP -ﬁltered, R−
P -equivariant chain
homotopy.
If L is a multi-based link in Y , with coloring σ, there is an identity decorated link cobordism
(Wid, Fσ
id): (Y, L) →(Y, L),
obtained by decorating ([0, 1] × Y, [0, 1] × L) with a vertical dividing set (see Deﬁnition (2.5) for a
precise deﬁnition).
Proving functoriality of the cobordism maps amounts to showing two things: that the identity
link cobordism induces the identity map, and that a version of the composition law is satisﬁed.
We note that the fact that (Wid, Fσ
id) induces the identity map is essentially immediate from the
deﬁnition. Indeed we can give (Wid, Fσ
id) a decomposition into elementary link cobordisms consisting
of a single elementary link cobordism, namely (Wid, Fσ
id) itself. The induced map is deﬁned to be
the identity.
As a second step towards proving functoriality, we prove the following version of the composition
law:
Theorem B. Suppose that (W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is a decorated link cobordism which decom-
poses as
(W, Fσ) = (W2, Fσ2
2 ) ∪(W1, Fσ1
1 ),
where each (Wi, Fσi
i ) is a decorated link cobordism, and σi = σ|Fi. If s1 and s2 are Spinc structures
on W1 and W2, respectively, then

1.2. Further remarks on the cobordism maps. We now make several brief remarks about the
TQFT described in this paper.
Firstly, we note that our TQFT is somewhat more ﬂexible than the original (3+1)-dimensional
TQFT described by Ozsv´ath and Szab´o [OS04b] [OS06] with respect to connectedness of 3- and 4-
manifolds. In this paper, we allow 3- and 4-manifolds to be disconnected or empty. Our requirements
for 3-manifolds containing multi-based links (Y, L) are simply that each connected component of Y
contains a component of L, and that each connected component of L contains at least two basepoints.
Similarly we require that each connected component of a 4-manifold contains a component of the
link cobordism surface, and that each connected component of the link cobordism surface contains
a dividing arc.
There are two elementary dividing sets on [0, 1] × L ⊆[0, 1] × Y which are particularly important
to our paper. They are shown in Figure 1.2. The induced maps are the quasi-stabilization maps,
which we denote by S+
w,z, S−
w,z, T +
w,z and T −
w,z. It is important to note that, up to isotopy, any
dividing set on [0, 1] × L can be built by stacking a sequence of such pieces on top of each other.
Hence all of our cobordism invariants for decorations of cylindrical link cobordisms can be encoded
into algebraic relations involving the quasi-stabilization maps; See Section 4 for further details.
S+
w,z
S−
w,z
z
w
T +
w,z
T −
w,z
z
w
Figure 1.2. Two simple dividing sets on [0, 1] × L ⊆[0, 1] × Y . In this paper,
we will write S+
w,z, S−
w,z, T +
w,z and T −
w,z for the induced maps, and refer to these
maps as the quasi-stabilization maps.
Finally, we remark that there is an additional relation which appears frequently in the TQFT.
Suppose (W, Fσ1

underlying, undecorated link cobordism (W, Σ), and whose decorations agree outside of a disk D2
contained in Σ. Suppose further that on D2, the three decorations satisfy the conﬁguration shown
in Figure 1.3. Then
(1)

Inspired by an analogous picture from contact geometry, we refer to the relation in Equation (1) as
the bypass relation. Since the bypass relation appears frequently, there are many ways of proving it.
See, e.g., Lemma 4.7.
F1

≃0
Figure 1.3. The bypass relation.
If three dividing sets on a decorated link
cobordism agree outside of a disk, and inside the disk they ﬁt into the triad shown,
then the sum of their invariants is chain homotopic to zero. Many relations between
naturally occurring maps on the link Floer complexes can be interpreted in terms
of this relation.

1.3. Algebraic reduction to the graph TQFT. As an application, we compare the link cobor-
dism maps deﬁned in this paper to the graph cobordism maps deﬁned in [Zem15] for cobordisms
(W, Γ) with embedded ribbon graphs between closed, multi-pointed 3-manifolds. For the purposes
of this section, we restrict to colorings with exactly two colors, and we further assume that all
the w-basepoints and regions are assigned the variable U, and all the z-basepoints and regions are
assigned the variable V . We omit the coloring σ from the notation in this section.
Deﬁnition 1.1. A ribbon graph cobordism (W, Γ): (Y1, w1) →(Y2, w2) between two multi-based
3-manifolds is a pair (W, Γ) such that the following are satisﬁed:
(1) W is a cobordism from Y1 to Y2.
(2) Γ is an embedded graph in W such that Γ ∩∂W = w1 ∪w2 and each basepoint of w1 and
w2 has valence 1 in Γ.
(3) Each vertex of Γ has valence at least 1.
(4) Γ is decorated with a ribbon structure, i.e., a choice of cyclic ordering of the edges adjacent
to each vertex.
In [Zem15], the author proves that there are Spinc functorial maps associated to ribbon graph
cobordisms (W, Γ) equipped with 4-dimensional Spinc structures.
If (Y, L) is a multi-based link in Y , then assuming that we are coloring the complexes with only
two variables U and V , as above, there are natural chain isomorphisms
CFL−(Y, L, s) ⊗F2[U,V ] F2[U, V ]/(V −1) ∼= CF −(Y, w, s)
and
CFL−(Y, L, s) ⊗F2[U,V ] F2[U, V ]/(U −1) ∼= CF −(Y, z, s −PD[L]).
The map FW,F,s associated to the link cobordism (W, F) thus induces two maps on CF −, for which
we write
FW,F,s|U=1
and
FW,F,s|V =1.
Given a decorated link cobordism (W, F): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2), we will describe a way to construct
ribbon graphs
Γ(Σw) ⊆Σw
and
Γ(Σz) ⊆Σz
which we will call ribbon 1-skeletons of the surfaces Σw and Σz, respectively (see Deﬁnition 14.5 for
a precise description). We prove the following:
Theorem C. Given a decorated link cobordism (W, F) with subsurfaces Σw and Σz and choices of
ribbon 1-skeletons Γ(Σw) and Γ(Σz), the reductions satisfy
FW,F,s|V =1 ≃F B
W,Γ(Σw),s
and
FW,F,s|U=1 ≃F A
W,Γ(Σz),s−PD[Σ].
In the above theorem, the maps F A
W,Γ,s are the graph cobordism maps deﬁned in [Zem15]. The
maps F B
W,Γ,s are minor variations, which we describe in Section 14. The two versions of the graph
cobordism maps (the A-maps and the B-maps) satisfy the relation
F B
W,Γ,s ≃F A
W,Γ,s,
where Γ denotes the ribbon graph Γ with cyclic orderings reversed.
In [Zem15], the graph cobordism maps are shown to be invariant under isotopies of the graph Γ
which ﬁx Γ ∩∂W. Many of the algebraic relations suggest that the maps are invariant under more
moves than just isotopy, such as adding trivial strands to the graph, or sliding edges across vertices
(while respecting the cyclic orderings). In Deﬁnition 14.4 we provide a notion of ribbon-equivalence
between ribbon graphs, which is weaker than isotopy. As corollaries to Theorem C, we obtain the
following:
Corollary D. If (W, Γ) and (W, Γ′) are ribbon-equivalent ribbon graph cobordisms, then
F A
W,Γ,s ≃F A
W,Γ′,s
and
F B
W,Γ,s ≃F B
W,Γ′,s.


===PDF PAGE 6===

Corollary E. The V = 1 reduction of FW,F,s,
FW,F,s|V =1 : CF −(Y1, w1, s|Y1) →CF −(Y2, w2, s|Y2),
depends only on the ribbon surface Σw and the Spinc structure s. The U = 1 reduction of FW,F,s,
FW,F,s|U=1 : CF −(Y1, z1, s −PD[L1]) →CF −(Y2, z2, s −PD[L2]),
depends only on the ribbon surface Σz, and the Spinc structure s −PD[Σ].
Remark 1.2. Corollary E can be used to give an alternate construction and proof of invariance of
the graph cobordism maps from [Zem15]; see Section 14.6.
1.4. Comparison to other link cobordism map constructions. There are already several
notable examples of link cobordism map constructions in the literature. The most relevant to this
paper is the construction by Juh´asz [Juh16] for the hat ﬂavor of link Floer homology, denoted [
HFL.
If L is a multi-pointed link in Y , the hat version of the link Floer complex, denoted d
CFL(Y, L), is
obtained by formally setting all of the variables in R−
P equal to 0, and then summing over all Spinc
structures. Taking homology, we obtain [
HFL(Y, L). We note that on the hat version of link Floer
homology, our maps are independent of the coloring σ. Similarly, Juh´asz’s deﬁnition of a decorated
link cobordism is equivalent to ours, if we ignore the coloring. In light of this, we make the following
conjecture:
Conjecture 1.3. The maps bFW,F := P
s∈Spinc(W ) bFW,F,s deﬁned in this paper for the hat ﬂavor
coincide with the maps FW,F deﬁned in [Juh16] using sutured Floer homology.1
In [JM18], Juh´asz and Marengon provide a partial computation of many elementary link cobordism
maps from Juh´asz’s sutured TQFT, which could be helpful in proving Conjecture 1.3. We will not
pursue the conjecture in this paper. We note that the maps induced by Juh´asz’s TQFT for knot
concordances are studied in [JM16].
There are additional partial constructions of link cobordism maps in link Floer homology. For
example, Sarkar deﬁnes maps associated to surfaces in [0, 1] × S3 in order to provide an alternate
proof of the Milnor conjecture using grid diagrams [Sar11].
Ozsv´ath, Stipsicz and Szab´o use a
grid diagrammatic construction of link cobordism maps to analyze the unoriented four-ball genus of
knots and links [OSS17]. We note that neither Sarkar nor Ozsv´ath–Stipsicz–Szab´o prove invariance
of their constructions. It would be interesting to know whether a construction and proof of invariance
could be done entirely through grid diagrams. A set of grid movie moves for decorated surfaces was
described in [Gra18], though veriﬁcation of the invariance of the grid homology maps under these
moves has not been completed. Nevertheless, we state the following conjecture:
Conjecture 1.4. The maps described in terms of grid diagrams by Sarkar [Sar11] and Ozsv´ath–
Stipsicz–Szab´o [OSS17] are equal to the maps deﬁned in this paper for elementary link cobordisms
inside of [0, 1] × S3.
There is an additional construction due to Alishahi and Eftekhary [AE16] of Floer homology for
multi-based tangles, generalizing knot Floer homology. Alishahi and Eftekhary give a construction
of cobordism maps for cobordisms between tangles, and prove invariance. When restricted to links,
their construction is similar to the one in this paper, and features maps associated to saddles which
are deﬁned with the same Heegaard triples as the ones in this paper.
Nonetheless, the TQFT
structure is not (obviously) the same as the one in this paper, though it is likely that there is a
concrete relationship.
1.5. Further developments. We now mention a few basic properties of the cobordism maps which
have been proven in subsequent papers. The link cobordism maps constructed in this paper satisfy
some simple Maslov and Alexander grading change formulas [Zem17b, Theorem 1.4]. Furthermore,
using the grading change formula, the author computes the maps for closed surfaces with simple
dividing sets when b1 = 0 and b+
2 = 0 [Zem17b, Theorem 1.7].
1Since the original posting of this paper, this conjecture has been proven by Juh´asz and the author [JZ18].


===PDF PAGE 7===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
7
The link cobordism maps also satisfy a version of conjugation invariance [Zem17a, Theorem 1.3]
similar to the version satisﬁed by the standard 4-manifold invariants [OS06, Theorem 3.6]. Using this,
and some further properties of the link cobordism maps, the author explores the relation between the
cobordism maps and the K¨unneth theorem for connected sums, and in particular proves a connected
sum formula for involutive knot Floer homology [Zem17a, Theorem 1.1].
1.6. Conventions and notation. Throughout the paper, we will use the outward normal ﬁrst
orientation convention, which is standard in gauge theory. That is, if M is an oriented manifold
and p ∈∂M, with an outward normal vector N at p, we say an ordered basis (v1, . . . , vn) of Tp∂M
is oriented if and only if (N, v1, . . . , vn) is an oriented basis for TpM. With this convention, the
identity cobordism is ([0, 1] × Y, [0, 1] × L).
Unless otherwise speciﬁed, we will write “chain homotopic” and use the symbol ≃to mean “chain
homotopic through ﬁltered and equivariant chain homotopies.”
Finally, whenever we list several consecutive basepoints on a link component, we will always write
them ordered from right to left (we will remind the reader of this convention). For example, if
we write (w, z, w′, z′), we mean the w follows z, which follows w′, which follows z′. We extend this
notation to quasi-stabilization maps and basepoint moving maps. If we write S+
w,z, the implication is
that w immediately follows z, whereas if we write S+
z′,w′, then the implication is that z′ immediately
follows w′.
1.7. Organization. In Section 2, we describe the categories of decorated link cobordisms and curved
chain complexes. In Section 3, we deﬁne the link Floer complexes we consider in this paper, and
prove some basic properties. In Section 4, we deﬁne the quasi-stabilization maps, as well as several
other maps which naturally appear on the link Floer complexes, and relate the quasi-stabilization
maps to some basepoint moving diﬀeomorphism maps on link Floer homology. In Section 5, we
describe the 4-dimensional handle maps on the link Floer complex. In Section 6, we deﬁne the
cobordism maps for saddle cobordisms. In Section 7, we describe some useful compositions of the
maps from the previous sections which are associated to several simple link cobordisms, such as a
birth or death link cobordism, or a 4-dimensional 1-handle containing a saddle. In Sections 8 and
9 we prove some more technical relations about the various maps deﬁned previously in the paper.
In Sections 10 and 11, we prove the necessary versions of Morse and Cerf theory which we will use
to prove invariance of the cobordism maps. In Section 12, we prove that the maps described in this
paper are invariants of decorated link cobordisms. In Section 13 we prove the composition law. In
Section 14, we show that the link cobordism maps naturally reduce to the graph cobordism maps.
1.8. Acknowledgments. The author would like to thank Akram Alishahi, Haofei Fan, Kristen
Hendricks, Jen Hom, Andr´as Juh´asz, Robert Lipshitz, Ciprian Manolescu, and Marco Marengon
for helpful conversations and suggestions. The author would also like to thank Sucharit Sarkar and
Andy Manion for suggesting to work in the setting of curved chain complexes. Finally, the author
thanks the anonymous referee for pointing out several errors and making many helpful suggestions.
2. The categories of decorated link cobordisms and curved chain complexes
In this section we deﬁne the category of decorated link cobordisms which features in this paper.
Deﬁnition 2.1. A 3-manifold with a multi-based link is a pair (Y, L) such that
(1) Y is a closed, oriented 3-manifold,
(2) L = (L, w, z) is a triple consisting of an oriented, embedded link L ⊆Y , with disjoint
collections of basepoints w and z on L, and
(3) each component of L has at least two basepoints, and the basepoints alternate between those
in w and those in z as one traverses a component of L.
Deﬁnition 2.2. A surface with divides is a pair F = (Σ, A) such that,
(1) Σ is a compact, oriented surface,
(2) A ⊆Σ is a properly embedded 1-manifold, and


===PDF PAGE 8===
8
IAN ZEMKE
(3) the components of Σ\A are partitioned into two sub-surfaces, Σw and Σz, which meet along
A.
To achieve well-deﬁned, functorial maps in our link TQFT, we need to consider colorings of the
basepoints and the surfaces with divides.
Deﬁnition 2.3. A coloring of a multi-based link L = (L, w, z) is a map σ: w ∪z →P , where P is
a ﬁnite set. A coloring of a surface with divides F = (Σ, A) is a map σ: C(Σ \ A) →P , where P is
a ﬁnite set, and C(Σ \ A) denotes the set of components of Σ \ A.
We think of P as the set of colors. We will write Lσ for a multi-based link equipped with a
coloring σ: w ∪z →P . Similarly we will write Fσ for a surface with divides, equipped with a
coloring σ. Note that the codomain P of σ is part of the data of a coloring.
Modeled on Juhasz’s deﬁnition of a decorated link cobordism [Juh16, Deﬁnition 4.5], we now
deﬁne a notion of morphism between 3-manifolds with multi-based links which we consider in this
paper:
Deﬁnition 2.4. If (Y1, L1) = (Y1, (L1, w1, z1)) and (Y2, L2) = (Y2, (L2, w2, z2)) are two 3-manifolds
with multi-based links, a decorated link cobordism from (Y1, L1) to (Y2, L2) consists of a pair (W, Fσ)
such that
(1) W is a compact 4-manifold with ∂W = −Y1 ⊔Y2,
(2) F = (Σ, A) is a surface with divides, equipped with the coloring σ,
(3) Σ ⊆W is a properly embedded, compact, oriented surface with Σ ∩∂W = −L1 ⊔L2,
(4) each component of Li \ A contains exactly one basepoint of wi ∪zi, and
(5) w1 ∪w2 ⊆Σw and z1 ∪z2 ⊆Σz.
We deﬁne the identity decorated link cobordism as follows:
Deﬁnition 2.5. If Y is a 3-manifold containing the multi-based link L = (L, w, z), equipped with
the coloring σ, the identity decorated link cobordism
(Wid, Fσ
id): (Y, L) →(Y, L)
is constructed by setting Wid := [0, 1] × Y and Fid := ([0, 1] × L, [0, 1] × p), where p ⊆L \ (w ∪z) is
a collection of points consisting of exactly one point per component of L \ (w ∪z). The coloring of
Fid (which by abuse of notation we are denoting σ) is the unique coloring which restricts to σ on L.
Remark 2.6. To properly deﬁne a link cobordism category, one must work with cobordisms which are
equipped with parametrizations of their boundary components. More precisely, a link cobordism
in the category of decorated link cobordisms consists of a 4-manifold with an embedded surface
(W, F) with boundary equal to (−Y ′
1, −L′
1)⊔(Y ′
2, L′
2), together with choices of orientation preserving
diﬀeomorphisms φi : (Y ′
i , L′
i) →(Yi, Li). Such a deﬁnition is necessary to precisely deﬁne a category
of cobordisms, since otherwise the cobordism category lacks identity morphisms, for example. We
will never explicitly deal with cobordisms at this level, since the notation becomes cumbersome.

2.1. The category of curved chain complexes. Suppose that R is a ring of characteristic two.
In this section we describe the category of curved chain complexes over R.
Deﬁnition 2.8. A curved chain complex over R is an R-module C, together with an endomorphism
∂: C →C such that
∂2 = ω · idC,
for some ω ∈R. The constant ω is called the curvature constant.
We now establish some terminology (which is standard in the context of genuine chain complexes):
Deﬁnition 2.9. We say F : (C1, ∂1) →(C2, ∂2) is a morphism of curved chain complexes over R
if F : C1 →C2 is an R-module homomorphism. We say a morphism F : (C1, ∂1) →(C2, ∂2) is a
homomorphism of curved chain complexes if F is also a chain map, i.e.,
F ◦∂1 = ∂2 ◦F.
An R-equivariant chain homotopy between two morphisms F, G: (C1, ∂1) →(C2, ∂2) is an R-module
homomorphism H : C →C′ such that F + G = ∂2 ◦H + H ◦∂1.
We note that there are not many interesting homomorphisms between curved chain complexes
with diﬀerent curvatures:
Lemma 2.10. Suppose that (C1, ∂1) and (C2, ∂2) are two curved chain complexes which are free
over a domain R, with curvature constants ω1 and ω2, respectively, and suppose that F : C1 →C2
is a homomorphism of curved chain complexes over R. If ω1 ̸= ω2, then F = 0.


===PDF PAGE 10===
10
IAN ZEMKE
Proof. Since F is a homomorphism of curved chain complexes, we have ∂2◦F = F ◦∂1. Precomposing
with ∂1, and using the fact that F is a chain map, we see that
∂2
2 ◦F = F ◦∂2
1,
so that (ω1 + ω2) · F = 0. Since C1 and C2 are free modules over the domain R, it follows that F is
the zero map.
□
Remark 2.11. In light of Lemma 2.10, one should not expect a TQFT to give meaningful chain maps
between curved chain complexes with diﬀerent curvatures. Lemma 2.7 implies that if two colored
links are cobordant via a decorated link cobordism, then their curvature constants are the same. In
Lemma 3.4, below, we identify the constant ωL with the curvatures of the link Floer chain complexes
CFL−(Y, L, s).
Given two curved chain complexes C1 = (C1, ∂1) and C2 = (C2, ∂2) over R (of arbitrary curvatures)
we can consider the curved complex of morphisms
MorR(C1, C2),
consisting of R-module homomorphisms from C1 to C2.
A diﬀerential on MorR(C1, C2) can be
deﬁned by the formula
∂Mor(f) = ∂2 ◦f + f ◦∂1.
Lemma 2.12. If C1 and C2 are two curved chain complexes over R, with curvatures ω1 and ω2,
then MorR(C1, C2) is a curved chain complex with curvature ω1 + ω2.
Proof. One simply computes
(∂Mor ◦∂Mor)(f) = ∂2
2 ◦f + ∂2 ◦f ◦∂1 + ∂2 ◦f ◦∂1 + f ◦∂2
1 = (ω1 + ω2) · f.
□
An important algebraic structure on the link Floer chain complexes is the ﬁltration. The standard
deﬁnition of a ﬁltered chain complex is suitable for our needs:
Deﬁnition 2.13. A Zn ﬁltration on a curved chain complex (C, ∂) over R is a collection of subgroups
GI ⊆C ranging over I ∈Zn such that ∂(GI) ⊆GI and such that if I ≤J (i.e., I(i) ≤J(i) for each
i ∈{1, . . . , n}) then GI ⊇GJ.
Suppose C1 = (C1, ∂1) and C2 = (C2, ∂2) are two curved chain complexes over R, which are also
both ﬁltered over Zn. Write GI(Ci) for the subgroups of Ci associated to their ﬁltrations. There is
an induced ﬁltration over Zn of the morphism complex MorR(C1, C2). If I ∈Zn is a multi-index,
then a ﬁltration on the morphism complex can be deﬁned by the formula
GI(MorR(C1, C2)) := {f ∈MorR(C1, C2) : f(GJ(C1)) ⊆GI+J(C2) for all J ∈Zn} .
Note that by Lemma 2.12, if C1 and C2 are curved chain complexes with the same curvatures,
then MorR(C1, C2) is a genuine chain complex. A homomorphism of curved chain complexes from
C1 to C2 is the same as a cycle in the morphism complex MorR(C1, C2). A ﬁltered map from C1 to
C2 is the same as an element in G0(MorR(C1, C2)), where 0 ∈Zn denotes the zero vector. A ﬁltered
chain map, deﬁned up to ﬁltered, R-equivariant chain homotopies, is the same as an element in the
homology group
H∗(G0(MorR(C1, C2))).
Remark 2.14. The link Floer complexes we consider in this paper will in general have non-zero
curvature. However, by choosing a coloring (σ, P ) of the links and decorated surfaces appropriately,
one can ensure that all the link Floer chain complexes are genuine chain complexes (i.e.
have
curvature constant 0 ∈R). For example, in the case that there are only two variables, one for the
w-basepoints and one for the z-basepoints, the curvature constant is zero. This situation is suﬃcient
for most of our applications. Nonetheless, many properties of the maps appearing in the link Floer
TQFT are more natural to consider in the context of curved chain complexes.


===PDF PAGE 11===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY

2.2. Transitive systems of curved chain complexes. We note that we cannot quite work on
just the level of chain complexes, since the modules CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) require several choices of auxiliary
data. To deﬁne the invariants CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) precisely, we need the following formalism:
Deﬁnition 2.15. If C is an category and A is a set, a transitive system over C indexed by A is a
map C : A →C, together with distinguished morphisms
Φa→a′ : C(a) →C(a′)
for a, a′ ∈A such that
• Φa→a = id;
• Φa′→a′′ ◦Φa→a′ = Φa→a′′.
If C1 and C2 are two transitive systems over C, indexed by A1 and A2 respectively, then a morphism
of transitive systems over C is a collection of morphisms Fa1,a2, indexed by pairs (a1, a2) ∈A1 × A2,
such that
Fa1,a2 : C1(a1) →C2(a2)
and the following diagram commutes for any two pairs (a1, a2), (a′
1, a′
2) ∈A1 × A2:
C1(a1)
C2(a2)
C1(a′
1)
C2(a′
2)
Fa1,a2
Φa1→a′
1
Φa2→a′
2
Fa′
1,a′
2
.
Note that a morphism between transitive systems is determined by a single one of the morphisms
Fa1,a2.
3. The link Floer complexes and some basic properties
In this section, we deﬁne the curved link Floer chain complexes CFL∞(Y, Lσ, s) which feature in
this paper.
3.1. Heegaard diagrams for multi-based links. We use the following standard deﬁnition of a
Heegaard diagram of a multi-based link:
Deﬁnition 3.1. If L = (L, w, z) is a multi-based link in Y 3, an embedded Heegaard diagram of
(Y, L) is a tuple (Σ, α, β, w, z) such that the following hold:
(1) Σ is an oriented, embedded surface in Y , which divides Y into two handlebodies, Uα and
Uβ.
(2) Σ is oriented as ∂Uα = −∂Uβ.
(3) Σ ∩L = w ∪z and L intersects Σ positively at the z-basepoints and negatively at the
w-basepoints.
(4) α consists of g(Σ) + |w| −1 pairwise disjoint, simple closed curves which bound a set of
g(Σ) + |w| −1 pairwise disjoint compressing disks Dα ⊆Uα \ L. The set Uα \ Dα consists
of |w| 3-balls, each of which contains a single w-basepoint and a single z-basepoint. An
analogous condition holds for the β curves.
(5) The arcs L ∩Uα can be isotoped within Uα \ Dα, relative to their endpoints, into Σ. The
arcs L ∩Uβ satisfy an analogous condition.
We remark that Condition (5) provides a way of visualizing the link. First, one connects the
z-basepoints to the w-basepoints in Σ \ α, and then pushes the interior of these arcs into Uα. Then
one connects the w-basepoints to the z-basepoints in Σ \ β, and pushes the interiors of these arcs
into Uβ. Connecting up these two collections of arcs yields the link L (up to isotopy).
Writing n for the quantity |w| = |z|, there are two embedded tori in Symg+n−1(Σ), namely
Tα = α1 × · · · × αg+n−1
and
Tβ = β1 × · · · × βg+n−1.


===PDF PAGE 12===
12
IAN ZEMKE
Given a Heegaard diagram (Σ, α, β, w, z), and an intersection point x ∈Tα ∩Tβ, we deﬁne the
set Πw
x to be the set of homotopy classes φ ∈π2(x, x) which have nw(φ) = 0 for all w ∈w. There
is a map
H : Πw
x →H2(Y ; Z),
obtained by coning oﬀa 2-chain φ using the α and β curves. Throughout the paper, we will restrict
attention to diagrams which satisfy the following admissibility condition (which is a slight adaptation
of [OS04b, Deﬁnition 4.10]):
Deﬁnition 3.2. A diagram H = (Σ, α, β, w, z) is strongly s-admissible if there is an intersection
point x ∈Tα ∩Tβ such that sw(x) = s and for any class φ ∈Πw
x with
⟨c1(s), H(φ)⟩= 2N > 0
the class φ has a multiplicity which is at least N.
The above admissibility condition implies that for a ﬁxed Spinc structure, the set of homotopy
classes with Maslov index j which have nonnegative domains is ﬁnite [OS04b, Lemma 4.14] (see also
[Zem15, Lemma 4.9] for an adaptation of their argument for multi-pointed diagrams).
3.2. 3-dimensional Spinc structures. As with the Heegaard Floer invariants of closed 3-manifolds
[OS04b], we use the Turaev interpretation of Spinc structures on closed 3-manifolds [Tur97]. More
precisely, we deﬁne Spinc(Y ) to be the collection of non-vanishing vector ﬁelds on Y modulo the
equivalence relation of being isotopic on the complement of a collection of 3-balls. The set Spinc(Y )
is an aﬃne space over H1(Y ; Z).
Ozsv´ath and Szab´o describe a way of associating a 3-dimensional Spinc structure to an intersection
point [OS04b]. If (Σ, α, β, w) is an n-pointed Heegaard diagram for Y , then there is a map
sw : Tα ∩Tβ →Spinc(Y ).
If x ∈Tα ∩Tβ is an intersection point, then sw(x) is constructed as follows: One ﬁrst picks a
Morse function which induces the diagram (Σ, α, β) and considers the gradient vector ﬁeld. Note
that such a Morse function will have |w| local minima, and |w| local maxima. Each point in x
determines a ﬂowline connecting an index 1 and index 2 critical point of the Morse function. The
basepoints w each determine a ﬂowline from an index 0 critical point to an index 3 critical point.
After removing regular neighborhoods of these ﬂowlines, we obtained a non-vanishing vector ﬁeld
on the complement of this neighborhood. We can extend this to a non-vanishing vector ﬁeld on all
of Y since the ﬂowlines we removed connected critical points with indices of opposite parity.
The map sw depends on the choice of basepoints, w, in our Heegaard diagram. The dependence
on the basepoints can be summarized as follows:
Lemma 3.3. Suppose (Σ, α, β, w, z) is a diagram for the multi-based link (Y, L, w, z).
If x ∈
Tα ∩Tβ, then
sw(x) −sz(x) = PD[L].
Proof. This follows from reasoning identical to the proof of [OS04b, Lemma 2.19]. Alternatively,
Spinc(Y ) is described as the set of homology classes of nonvanishing vector ﬁelds on Y . Given a
closed path γ ∈H1(Y ; Z), the action of γ ∈H1(Y ; Z), can be described by a procedure sometimes
referred to as Reeb surgery (cf. [Nic03, Section 3.2]). Given the explicit description from [OS04b]
of the vector ﬁelds sw and sz, it is easy to see that they diﬀer by Reeb surgery on the link L.
□
It follows from the previous lemma that if the total class of a link is trivial in H1(Y ; Z), then the
two maps sw and sz agree. Note that we could instead have deﬁned our link Floer complexes to be
generated by intersection points x with sz(x) = s. For a link whose total class is non-zero, this does
not change the resulting link Floer complexes. For links whose total class is not null-homologous, the
resulting complexes may be diﬀerent. An instructive example is Y = S1 × S2 with K = S1 × {pt}.


===PDF PAGE 13===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY

An important property of Heegaard Floer homology is that it is independent of the choice of
Heegaard diagram. For our purposes, we will need the following naturality result:


===PDF PAGE 14===
14
IAN ZEMKE
Proposition 3.5. Given two strongly s-admissible diagrams H and H′ for (Y, L), there is a Zw⊕Zz-
ﬁltered, F2[Uw, Vz]-equivariant chain homotopy equivalence
ΦH→H′ : CFL−(H, s) →CFL−(H′, s),
which is well-deﬁned up to ﬁltered, equivariant chain homotopies.
Proof. In the setting of the knot and link invariants [OS04a] [OS08], Ozsv´ath and Szab´o constructed
maps associated to changes of the almost complex structure, isotopies and handleslides of the α and
β curves, stabilizations of the Heegaard surface, as well as isotopies of the Heegaard surface relative
to L. They showed that these maps were quasi-isomorphisms. If H and H′ are arbitrary diagrams,
one can construct a transition map ΦH→H′ as the composition of the maps associated to a sequence
of elementary Heegaard moves connecting H and H′. Juh´asz and Thurston [JT12] showed that the
maps ΦH→H′ are independent, up to chain homotopy, on the choice of intermediate diagrams. We
note that neither Ozsv´ath–Szab´o nor Juh´asz–Thurston explicitly showed that these transition maps
are chain homotopy equivalences, as opposed to quasi-isomorphisms. To see that the maps are chain
homotopy equivalences, we refer the reader to [HM17, Proposition 2.3], whose proof works equally
well in our setting.
□
In light of the above naturality result, we deﬁne CFL∞(Y, L, s) to be the transitive system over C
indexed by A (cf. Deﬁnition 2.15), where A is the set of all strongly s-admissible diagrams for (Y, L)
and C is the category of Zw ⊕Zz-ﬁltered, F2[Uw, Vz]-equivariant curved chain complexes. We call
CFL∞(Y, L, s) the transitive chain homotopy type invariant.
Finally, we note that for some of the more technical arguments of the paper, we will use Lipshitz’s
cylindrical reformulation of Heegaard Floer homology [Lip06].
Instead of counting holomorphic
curves from the complex disk into Symn+g−1(Σ), Lipshitz’s version of Heegaard Floer homology
counts pseudo-holomorphic curves of higher genus which map into Σ × [0, 1] × R. The equivalence
of Lipshitz’s reformulation with the Ozsv´ath–Szab´o construction follows (at least morally) from the
tautological correspondence between holomorphic disks mapping into Symn+g−1(Σ) and holomorphic
curves mapping into Σ × [0, 1] × R, whose projection onto [0, 1] × R are (n + g −1)-fold branched
covering maps. We refer the reader to [Lip06] for more technical details about this approach.
3.4. Coloring the link Floer complexes. Suppose that
σ: w ∪z →P
is a coloring of L = (L, w, z). The coloring σ induces an F2[Uw, Vz]-module structure on R−

deﬁne the colored complexes
CFL−(H, σ, s) := CFL−(H, s) ⊗F2[Uw,Vz] R−

CFL∞(H, σ, s) := CFL∞(H, s) ⊗F2[Uw,U −1

The complexes CFL∞(H, σ, s) ﬁt together to form a transitive system over C, indexed by A, where
C is the category of ZP -ﬁltered, curved chain complexes over R∞
P , and A is the set of strongly
s-admissible Heegaard diagrams for (Y, L). Note that the ﬁltration on CFL∞(H, σ, s) is given by
ﬁltering by powers of the variables in R∞
P .
We note that including colorings is essential for functoriality. Without colorings, we do not have
a ﬁxed category to work in. Also, most of the maps we deﬁne for decorations on the surface are not
chain maps until we color the complexes.
Although we will primarily work with curved chain complexes, we will occasionally need to work
on the level of homology. If Lσ is a colored link with ωL,σ = 0 ∈F2[UP ] (or equivalently ∂2 = 0 on
CFL◦(Y, Lσ, s)), we deﬁne
HFL◦(Y, Lσ, s) := H∗(CFL◦(Y, Lσ, s)).

3.5. Distinguished elements of HFL−for multi-based unlinks in (S1×S2)#n. Suppose Y is a
3-manifold with link L = (L, w, z), and σ is a coloring of L such that σ(w)∩σ(z) = ∅. Furthermore,
suppose that the total class of L in H1(Y ; Z) is null-homologous, and that s is a torsion Spinc
structure on Y . In this situation, the relative gradings grw and grz described in Equation (2) are
both well-deﬁned on the complex CFL−(Y, Lσ, s). If o ∈{w, z} we will let Maxgro(HFL−(Y, Lσ, s))
denote the subset of HFL−(Y, Lσ, s) of maximal gro grading.
Lemma 3.6. Suppose U is an unlink in (S1×S2)#n, and each component has exactly two basepoints.

To see that Θ is a cycle, one simply analyzes the proof of [OS04b, Lemma 9.1]. Using the relative

It is easy to see that on the diagram H, the only disks in any π2(Θ, y) which do not cross over any

We now consider the case that all components of the unlink U ⊆(S1 × S2)#g except for one have
exactly two basepoints, and that the remaining component of U has exactly four basepoints. These
will appear when we deﬁne maps associated to saddle cobordisms.
Lemma 3.7. Suppose that U is an unlink in (S1 × S2)#n such that all components of U except for
one have exactly two basepoints, and one component of U has exactly four basepoints. Let U denote

the set of colors P0 := w ∪(z/(z ∼z′)) where z/(z ∼z′) denotes the set of z-basepoints modulo the
relation that z ∼z′, and deﬁne the coloring σ0 : w ∪z →P0 to be the natural map. Then there an

Proof. We will prove the claim about Maxgrw(HFL−), since the result about Maxgrz(HFL−) fol-

nient diagram H for ((S1 × S2)#n, U). We start with a diagram H0 = (S2, α0, β0, w, w′, z, z′) for
(S3, U, w, w′, z, z′), obtained taking the Heegaard surface S2 ⊆S3, and picking an α0 and β0 curve
which intersect in two points.
There are four components of S2 \ (α0 ∪β0), all of which are bigons. Each bigon contains one
of the basepoints w, w′, z and z′. We can form a diagram H for ((S1 × S2)#n, U) by attaching
a genus n surface Σn to S2 in the bigon on S2 which contains the basepoint w. On Σn, we can
pick additional attaching curves α and β which are small Hamiltonian translates of each other,
intersecting in pairs of points. We can place the additional basepoints on Σn so that they come in
pairs of w and z-basepoints, which are immediately adjacent to each other. An example is shown
in Figure 3.1.
There is an intersection point Θw of maximal grw grading on H. The same argument that we used
in the proof of Lemma 3.6 shows that sums of elements of the form Θw · V J

CFL−(H, s) of top grw grading. Also as before, we note that none of these elements are boundaries.
However, it is important to note that on the uncolored module CFL−(H, s0), the element Θw is
not a cycle. Instead ∂(Θw) = (Vz +Vz′)·Θz, as shown in Figure 3.1. On the other hand, the coloring
σ0 identiﬁes Vz and Vz′, so Θw becomes a cycle in the colored chain complex. Hence

 CFL−(H, σ0, s)

 CFL−(H, s)


which is clearly isomorphic to F2[Vz]/(Vz −Vz′).
□
4. Quasi-stabilization, basepoint actions, and basepoint moving maps
In this section, we describe some important maps which appear in the graph TQFT.
4.1. Quasi-stabilization maps for adding and removing basepoints. In this section, we de-
scribe the quasi-stabilization operation, initially described in [MO10], which is a procedure for adding
or removing an adjacent pair of basepoints from a link component. Some basic properties of the
quasi-stabilization operation (such as the fact that the quasi-stabilization maps are chain maps, and
induce natural chain maps between link Floer complexes) are proven in [Zem17c].


===PDF PAGE 17===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
17
w
z
w′
z′
Figure 3.1. A diagram H for ((S1 × S2)#n, U) in Lemma 3.7. The intersection
point Θw is labeled with solid dots. The two shaded bigons are disks contributing
to the relation ∂(Θw) = (Vz + Vz′) · Θz.
Suppose that w and z are new basepoints for a link L = (L, w, z), contained in a single component
of L\(w ∪z), such that w immediately follows z with respect to the links orientation. Suppose that
σ: w ∪z →P is a coloring and that σ′ : w ∪z ∪{w, z} →P is a coloring which extends σ. Suppose
also that z is given the same color as the other z-basepoint adjacent to w. We will describe ﬁltered
equivariant chain maps
S+
w,z : CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL−(Y, (L+
w,z)σ′, s)
and
S−

w,z)σ′, s) →CFL−(Y, Lσ, s),
which are well-deﬁned on the transitive chain homotopy type invariants. Here L+
w,z denotes the
multi-based link (L, w ∪{w}, z ∪{z}). If instead z follows w, there are maps S+
z,w and S−
z,w, which
are deﬁned analogously. We will call these the type-S quasi-stabilization maps.
Remark 4.1. Our notation for the quasi-stabilization follows the right-to-left convention described
in Section 1.6. If we write S◦
w,z, the implication is that w immediately follows z. If we write S◦
z′,w′,
the implication is that z′ immediately follows w′.
The construction of the type-S quasi-stabilization map is not symmetric between the w and the
z-basepoints. Instead, by reversing the roles of w and z-basepoints in the construction, we will
construct type-T quasi-stabilization maps T +
w,z and T −
w,z as well. If σ: w∪z →P is a coloring which
is extended by σ′ : w ∪z ∪{w, z} →P such that w is given the same color as the other w basepoint
adjacent to z, we will describe ﬁltered, equivariant chain maps
T +
w,z : CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL−(Y, (L+
w,z)σ′, s)
and
T −
w,z : CFL−(Y, (L+
w,z)σ′, s) →CFL−(Y, Lσ, s).
We now provide the description of both the type-S and type-T quasi-stabilization maps on the
level of intersection points and Heegaard diagrams.
Suppose we are given a Heegaard diagram H = (Σ, α, β, w, z) for (Y, L), and w and z are new
basepoints on L, which are contained in a single component of L \ (w ∪z). Furthermore, suppose
that w comes after z. Write w′ and z′ for the two basepoints of L which are adjacent to w and
z. Since w appears immediately after z on L, if follows from Deﬁnition 3.1 that the component of
L \ (w ∪z) which contains w and z is contained in the Uα handlebody of Y \ Σ.
Let A denote the component of Σ\α containing w′ and z′. We pick a point p ∈A\(α∪β ∪w∪z)
and a choice of new αs curve in A passing through p which cuts A into two pieces, one of which
contains w′ and one of which contains z′. Note that αs can in general intersect many diﬀerent β
curves.
Let H0 = (D2, α0, β0, w, z) be a piece of a Heegaard diagram, where D2 is a disk, α0 is an arc
with both endpoints on ∂D2, which cuts D2 into two pieces, one of which contains w and one of
which contains z. Furthermore β0 is a closed curve which intersects α0 twice and bounds a disk


===PDF PAGE 18===
18
IAN ZEMKE
containing the basepoints w and z. We then paste H0 onto H at p, so that α0 is lined up with αs.
This operation is called the special connected sum in [MO10]. An example of a quasi-stabilization
is shown in Figure 4.1.

Note that αs ∩β0 consists of two points, which are distinguished by the gradings grw and grz.

redundantly, we will write θz for the higher grz-graded intersection point, and ξz for the lower. By

and

If w and z are two new adjacent basepoints, but z follows w, then a slight adaptation of the above
construction yields maps S+
z,w, S−
z,w, T +
z,w and T −
z,w. In this situation, if we let w′ and z′ denote
the two basepoints of L which are adjacent to w and z, then w′ and z′ are contained in a single
component B ⊆Σ \ β. The quasi-stabilization maps are then deﬁned in this case by picking a point
p ∈B \ (α ∪β ∪{w′, z′}) and a simple closed curve βs ⊆B \ β which intersects p and separates
w′ from z′. The quasi-stabilization construction is then performed by inserting a new α0 curve
intersecting βs twice, and bounding the new basepoints w and z. The same formulas as before are
used to deﬁne the maps S+
z,w, S−
z,w, T +
z,w and T −
z,w.
Remark 4.2. The quasi-stabilization maps are only deﬁned when w and z are not the only basepoints
on their link component, since link Floer homology is only deﬁned when all components of a link
have at least two basepoints.
The ﬁrst step towards analyzing the quasi-stabilization maps is to understand when they are chain
maps. In [Zem17c], the following is proven using a neck-stretching argument:

We now summarize several important properties of the maps Φw and Ψz. By diﬀerentiating the
expression ∂2 = ωL · id from Lemma 3.4 once, with respect to a variable in F2[Uw, Vz], we obtain
the following:
Lemma 4.6 ([Zem17c, Lemma 3.1]). If z is adjacent to w and w′ then
∂Ψz + Ψz∂= Uw + Uw′.
If w is adjacent to z and z′ then we have that
∂Φw + Φw∂= Vz + Vz′.
In particular, the maps Φw and Ψz are not chain maps on the uncolored complexes. In fact, Ψz is
a chain map if and only if the variables Uw and Uw′ are identiﬁed, and Φw is a chain map if and only
if the variables Vz and Vz′ are identiﬁed. Notice that this corresponds exactly to the condition that
the coloring of the basepoints is compatible with a coloring of the decorated surfaces from Figure 4.2.
The following is easily proven by diﬀerentiating the relations in Lemma 4.6 with respect to one of
the Uw or Vz variables:
Lemma 4.7 ([Zem17c, Lemmas 9.1 and 9.2]). If w, w′, z and z′ are basepoints on L, we have
ΦwΦw′ + Φw′Φw ≃0,
and
ΨzΨz′ + Ψz′Ψz ≃0.
We also have
ΦwΨz + ΨzΦw + N(w, z) · id ≃0,
where N(w, z) ∈F2 is the number of components of L\(w∪z) which have both w and z as boundary.
Interpretations of the relations from Lemma 4.6 in terms of dividing sets on cylindrical link
cobordisms are shown in Figure 4.3.
Remark 4.8. it is often convenient to think of the maps Φw as being (d/dUw)(∂). Note that there

between the two operations is reﬂected by the Leibniz rule for diﬀerentiating products. Hence on

Lemma 4.16. Suppose that (L, w, z) is a multi-based link in Y and (w, z) is an adjacent pair of

the unstabilized link) which are adjacent to z′.

In [Zem17c], we showed the following commutation result about quasi-stabilization maps:
Proposition 4.18 ([Zem17c, Theorem 8.1]). If (w, z) and (w′, z′) are two disjoint pairs of base-

w,z,

and (w′, z′) appear with the opposite ordering (i.e. if z follows w and z′ follows w′, and we use the
maps S◦
z,w and S◦′
z′,w′, instead).

carries over without major change if we replace both S-quasi-stabilizations by T-quasi-stabilizations:
Proposition 4.19. If (w, z) and (w′, z′) are disjoint pairs of basepoints, with w following z and w′
following z′, then

maps T ◦

from a small modiﬁcation of the argument used to show that the type-S quasi-stabilization maps
commute with each other [Zem17c, Section 8].
We start with a diagram H = (Σ, α, β, w, z) for the unstabilized link L = (L, w, z). We form the
doubly quasi-stabilized diagram H++ by inserting the two subdiagrams shown in Figure 4.11 at two
points p and p′ in Σ \ α.
The diagrams H, H++, and the two intermediate, singly quasi-stabilized diagrams can be used to
compute all of the quasi-stabilization maps. Although the formulas for the two quasi-stabilization
maps look like they commute, it is important to note that we place very speciﬁc requirements on the
almost complex structures which can be used to compute the quasi-stabilization maps. To compute
S◦′
w′,z′, the almost complex structure must be stretched on the curve c′ shown in Figure 4.11. To
compute T ◦
w,z, one must stretch along the curve c. To compute the commutator of the two maps, we
must compute a change of almost complex structure map associated to changing the relative lengths
of the two necks. If Js is an almost complex structure on Σ × [0, 1] × R which is split in a cylindrical

We let {Ti

neighborhoods of the set {p, p′} whose intersection over i ∈N is {p, p′}. Also Js is a ﬁxed cylindrical
almost complex structure on Σ × [0, 1] × R.
Given a sequence of eJi-holomorphic disks ui, representing a Maslov index 0 homology class φ on
H++, we can extract a broken collection of limiting holomorphic curves on (Σ × [0, 1] × R, Js). As
in the proof of [MO10, Proposition 6.2], we can arrange the limiting curves on (Σ × [0, 1] × R, Js)
into a broken holomorphic disk U0 on (Σ, α, β), which has no boundary components mapping to αs
or α′

s. We
will write φ0 for the total homology class of U0.
There are two cases to consider:
(1) p and p′ are in diﬀerent components, Ap and Ap′, of Σ \ α.
(2) p and p′ are in the same component, Ap,p′, of Σ \ α.
Consider Case (1) ﬁrst. This occurs exactly when (w, z) and (w′, z′) are not adjacent on L. In
this case, following the proof of [Zem17c, Lemma 8.3], the Maslov index µ(φ) satisﬁes the formula
µ(φ) = µ(φ0) + n1(φ) + n2(φ) + n′

Since φ0 has the broken holomorphic representative U0, we know that µ(φ0) ≥0, by transversality.

terms must be zero. It is easy to see that this implies that φ is a constant homology class. On

follows that if T1 and T2 are two pairs of neck lengths, all of whose components are suﬃciently
large, then ΦJs(T1)→Js(T2) is the identity map, on the level of intersection points. In particular, it
follows that S◦′
w′,z′ and T ◦

Consider now Case (2), which is slightly more subtle. This case occurs when the pairs (w, z) and
(w′, z′) are adjacent on L. By hypotheses on the non-adjacency of z and w′, the four basepoints
must appear with ordering (w′, z′, w, z), read right to left.
In this case, the curves αs and α′

The homology class can be described as a bigon in each of the quasi-stabilization regions glued to a

counted. Hence it follows that if we write F = ΦJs(T1)→Js(T2) then
F(x × ξz × (θw)′) = x × ξz × (θw)′,
F(x × ξz × (ξw)′) = x × ξz × (ξw)′ + C · x × θz × (θw)′,
F(x × θz × (θw)′) = x × θz × (θw)′,
F(x × θz × (ξw)′) = x × θz × (ξw)′,
(6)
for some C ∈F2 (which is not independent of T1 and T2). Note that the map F = ΦJs(T1)→Js(T2)

P .

4.4. Quasi-stabilization and basepoint moving maps. In this section, we describe some useful
formulas involving the quasi-stabilization maps and the diﬀeomorphism maps on link Floer homology
induced by moving basepoints.
The relations in this section generalize the relations for moving
basepoints from [Zem17c].
We recall that we constructed quasi-stabilization maps for adding a new pair of adjacent basepoints
(w, z). The exact construction diﬀered depending on the relative ordering of w and z. If w followed
z, we write S◦
w,z and T ◦
w,z. If instead z follows w, then we write S◦
z,w and T ◦
z,w. Analyzing our
proposed interpretation in terms of dividing sets, one should expect the two constructions to be
related by a diﬀeomorphism map. In this section, we prove a precise relation.
Suppose that L = (L, w, z0 ∪{z′}) is a multi-based link. Suppose that (w, z) is a new pair of
adjacent basepoints, which are contained in a single component of L \ (w ∪z0 ∪{z′}), such that z′
is adjacent to w and immediately follows w with respect to the links orientation. Let τ z′→z be a
diﬀeomorphism
(7)
τ z′→z : (Y, L, w, z0 ∪{z′}) →(Y, L, w, z0 ∪{z})
such that τ z′→z(z′) = z, and τ z′→z is the identity outside of a neighborhood of the arc between z′
and z. Deﬁne the diﬀeomorphism τ z′←z to be the inverse of τ z′→z.
Remark 4.22. Our notation for the basepoint moving maps follows the right-to-left convention de-
scribed in Section 1.6. Hence the notation τ z′→z and τ z′←z encodes the fact that z′ immediately
follows z with respect to the link’s orientation.
Lemma 4.23. There are chain homotopies
S+
w,z ≃S+
z′,wτ z′→z
∗
and
S−

Proof. We will focus on the relation S+

∗

argument.
We consider the diagram shown in Figure 4.13, which can be used to compute both S+

w,z,
we need to stretch along the circle cβ, encircling β0, whereas to compute S+
z′,w we need to stretch
along the curve cα, encircling the curve α0. The argument will proceed by analyzing a change of
almost complex structure map between an almost complex structure stretched along c and cα and
an almost complex structure stretched along c and cβ.
We pick a cylindrical almost complex structure Js on Σ×[0, 1]×R which is split in a neighborhood
of z′ on Σ. We construct the cylindrical almost complex structure Jα on Σ × [0, 1] × R by cutting
out a neighborhood of z′, and gluing in an almost complex structure on D2 × [0, 1] × R, for a disk
D2 containing the diagram shown in Figure 4.13. Furthermore, we assume that Jα has long necks
inserted along c and cα. Similarly we construct an almost complex structure Jβ which is stretched
along c and cβ.
We make the following two subclaims:
Subclaim (1): If Jα and J′

We note that a more detailed analysis would allow one to strengthen Subclaim (1), by proving
that the ∗component of ΦJα→J′α can be taken to be 0, however we will not have need for this result.
The ∗component in ΦJα→Jβ cannot be taken to be 0 in general, however.
The proofs of both Subclaims (1) and (2) follow the same line of reasoning, so we focus on
Subclaim (2). The argument is similar to the proof of Proposition 4.20. Suppose we take sequences
of almost complex structures {Jα,i}i∈N and {Jβ,i}i∈N, such that the neck lengths of Jα,i along c and
cα approach +∞, and the neck lengths of Jβ,i along c and cβ approach +∞as well. We can pick
a sequence non-cylindrical almost complex structures eJi, interpolating Jα,i and Jβ,i, such that the
(Σ × [0, 1] × R, eJi) contains the almost complex submanifold
((Σ \ Ni) × [0, 1] × R, Js),
for a nested sequence of open neighborhoods Ni ⊆Σ such that T
i∈N Ni = {p}, for some point
p ∈Σ. Also, importantly, we can do this for a ﬁxed cylindrical almost complex structure Js on
Σ × [0, 1] × R. We will compute the map ΦJα,i→Jβ,i by counting index 0 holomorphic disks with the
almost complex structure eJi.
Given a class φ = φΣ#φ0 ∈π2(x × x, y × y) with φΣ ∈π2(x, y) a class on (Σ, α, β) and φ0 ∈
π2(x, y) a class on (S2, α0, β0), the Maslov index of φ is easily computed to be
(8)
µ(φ) = µ(φΣ) + grw(x, y) + 2nw(φ0),
where grw(x, y) is the drop in grw grading from x to y.
Given a sequence of eJi-holomorphic curves ui representing a class φ = φΣ#φ0, we can extract a
limit to a broken curve UΣ representing φΣ (note that technically a limiting collection of curves would
also contain curves on (S2, α0, β0) though such curves are not important for our present argument).
In particular, the class φΣ would have a broken holomorphic representative, and hence µ(φΣ) ≥0.
Hence, Equation (8) implies that if grw(x, y) > 0, then µ(φΣ) ≤−1, so there are no holomorphic
representatives. This implies that the lower left entry of the matrix for ΦJα→Jβ is 0. Similarly
if grw(x, y) = 0, then µ(φΣ) ≤0. By transversality and the existence of a broken holomorphic
representative of φΣ, we conclude that µ(φΣ) = 0, so φΣ is the constant class. Furthermore, nw(φ0) =
0. It is easy to see that this implies that φ must be a constant class. Conversely, the constant
homology classes always have representatives for eJi, which are counted by ΦJα→Jβ.
Hence the
diagonal entries of the change of almost complex structure map are identiﬁed with the identity map.


===PDF PAGE 33===

The argument establishing the form of ΦJα→J′α is essentially the same.

constant homology classes, and is thus the identity map on intersection points.

4.5. Quasi-stabilization and Heegaard triples. In this section, we state a useful result about
quasi-stabilization and triangle maps, from [MO10].
Given a Heegaard triple (Σ, α, β, γ), there is a 4-manifold Xα,β,γ, described by Ozsv´ath and
Szab´o [OS06, Section 2.2]. Let ∆denote a 2-cell, viewed as a triangle with boundary edges labeled
eα, eβ and eγ. If τ ∈{α, β, γ}, write Uτ for the 3-dimensional handlebody which has boundary
−Σ and has τ as a collection of compressing disks. The 4-manifold Xα,β,γ is deﬁned as
Xα,β,γ := ((∆× Σ) ⊔(eα × Uα) ⊔(eβ × Uβ) ⊔(eγ × Uγ)) /∼,
where ∼denotes gluing eτ × Uτ to ∆× Σ in the natural way.
Given a Heegaard triple (Σ, α, β, γ, w, z), as well as a curve αs ⊆Σ \ α and a distinguished point
p ∈αs \ (β ∪γ), we can construct a quasi-stabilized Heegaard triple T +
αs,p, as shown in Figure 4.18.
Note that the 4-manifolds Xα,β,γ and Xα∪{αs},β∪{β0},γ∪{γ0} are canonically diﬀeomorphic, since
both are constructed by gluing Σ×∆together with the handlebodies Uα ×eα, Uβ ×eβ and Uγ ×eγ,
and the Heegaard surface and the 3-dimensional handlebodies Uα, Uβ and Uγ are unchanged when
we quasi-stabilize. Furthermore, it is straightforward to see that if ψ ∈π2(x, y, z) is a homology class
of triangles on the triple (Σ, α, β, γ) and ψ0 is a triangle on the subdiagram (S2, αs, β0, γ0) which
has the same multiplicity on both sides of αs at the connected sum point, then sw(ψ#ψ0) = sw(ψ)
(note however that not all homology classes of triangles on T +
p,αs can be written as ψ#ψ0 for such
triangles ψ and ψ0).
If Js is an almost complex structure on Σ × [0, 1] × R which is split in a neighborhood of p, we
will write Js(T) for an almost complex structure obtained from Js by inserting a suﬃciently large
neck along the curve c, shown in Figure 4.18.
We will write (abusing notation slightly)
αs ∩β0 = {θw, θz},
αs ∩γ0 = {θw, θz}
and
β0 ∩γ0 = {θ+, θ−},
In the above expressions, if o ∈{w, z}, then we write θo for the top gro-graded intersection point
when the designation as top and bottom degree intersection point depends on the choice of grading.
We write θ+ for the top graded intersection point (when the designation is independent of the choice
of grading).

Remark 4.29. Proposition 4.28 was used [Zem17c] in order to prove that the quasi-stabilization maps
are natural. It implies that the quasi-stabilization maps S◦
w,z and T ◦
w,z commute with handleslides
and isotopies of the β curves on the unstabilized diagram. However Proposition 4.28 is not suﬃcient
to show that the quasi-stabilization maps commute with moves of the α curves, or are independent
of the choice of αs curve.
To fully show naturality of the quasi-stabillization maps, one needs
an additional triangle count [Zem17c, Theorem 6.5], which has a non-trivial requirement on the
unstabilized Heegaard triple (Σ, α, β, γ).
5. Maps for 4-dimensional handles
In this section we deﬁne maps for 4-dimensional handles attached in Y \L. The maps we describe
in this section are modiﬁcations of the maps from [OS06], though there are some notable diﬀerences.
5.1. Framed spheres and handle attachment cobordisms.
Deﬁnition 5.1. A framed k-sphere in Y \ L is a map
S: Sk × D3−k →Y \ L
which is an embedding.
Given a framed k-sphere S in Y \ L, we deﬁne the surgery of S to be the 3-manifold
Y (S) := (Y \ int(im(S))) ∪(Dk+1 × S3−k−1).
Note that the link L ⊆Y induces a link L ⊆Y (S). We deﬁne the trace of S to be the 4-manifold
W(Y, S) := ([0, 1] × Y ) ∪Hk+1
where
Hk+1 := Dk+1 × D3−k
is attached to {1} × im(S) ⊆{1} × Y . We deﬁne the trace link cobordism of S to be the undecorated
link cobordism
W(Y, L, S) := (W(Y, S), [0, 1] × L): (Y, L) →(Y (S), L).
If S is instead a collection of framed spheres in Y \ L, with pairwise disjoint images, we can
still form the surgered manifold Y (S) and the trace link cobordism W(Y, L, S), by performing the
surgeries or handle attachments simultaneously.
Note that although S−1×D4 is the empty set, it is useful to distinguish between a (−1)-dimensional
sphere S−1, and the framed link with no components S∅. We deﬁne the trace of S∅to be [0, 1] × Y ,
while we deﬁne the trace of a (−1)-dimensional sphere S−1 to be ([0, 1] × Y ) ⊔D4.


===PDF PAGE 38===
38
IAN ZEMKE
5.2. 0-handle and 4-handle maps. We now describe handle attachment maps for 0-handles and
4-handles, which we think of as the maps for framed (−1)- and 3-dimensional spheres in Y \ L. In
terms of decorated link cobordisms, the 0-handle map will be the cobordism map for a decorated
link cobordism (W, F) with F = (Σ, A), where W has a Morse function f such that there is a point
p ∈int(W) which is the unique critical point of f, f|Σ and f|A, and has index 0 for all three. The
4-handle map will correspond to a similar cobordism, but turned upside.
Suppose that (Y, L) is a 3-manifold containing a multi-based link, and let (S3, U) denote a copy of
S3 containing a doubly based unknot. Write L = (L, w, z) and U = (U, w, z), and let (Σ, α, β, w, z)
be a diagram for (Y, L). Let (S2, w, z) denote a diagram for (S3, U) which has no α- or β-curves.
Suppose that σ: w ∪z →P is a coloring, and σ′ : w ∪z ∪{w, z} →P is a coloring which extends σ.
We now describe the 0-handle map
FY,L,S−1,ˆs : CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL−(Y ⊔S3, (L ⊔U)σ′, s ⊔s0).
Here ˆs is the unique Spinc structure on [0, 1] × Y ∪D4 which extends s.
Noting that the set of intersection points on the diagrams (Σ, α, β) and (Σ ⊔S2, α, β) are equal,
we deﬁne the map
FY,L,S−1,ˆs : CFL−(Σ, α, β, w, z, σ, s ⊔s0) →CFL−(Σ ⊔S2, α, β, w, z, σ′, s ⊔s0)
via the formula
(12)
FY,L,S−1,ˆs(x) = x.
The map FY,L,S−1,ˆs is a chain map and commutes with the change of diagrams maps for Y .
Dually, a framed 3-sphere in a 3-manifold consists of a distinguished component of Y which is
identiﬁed with S3. If this copy of S3 contains a doubly based unknot U, we can deﬁne the 4-handle
map
FY,L,S3,ˆs : CFL−(Y ⊔S3, (L ⊔U)σ′, s ⊔s0) →CFL−(Y, Lσ, s)
using the inverse of the formula for the 0-handle in Equation (12).
5.3. 1-handle and 3-handle maps. We now describe the 1-handle and 3-handle maps.
The
constructions we present are similar but not identical to the 1-handle and 3-handle maps deﬁned
in [OS06]. See also the construction of 1-handle and 3-handle maps from [Juh16]. If (Y, L) is a
3-manifold with a multi-based link, a 1-handle is the trace cobordism of a framed 0-sphere in Y .
Similarly a 3-handle is the trace cobordism of a framed 2-sphere.
Suppose Si is a framed i-sphere in Y \ L, for i ∈{0, 2}. Given a Spinc structure s on Y , there is
a unique Spinc structure ˆs on the trace W(Y, Si) which extends s. We let s′ denote the restriction
of ˆs to Y (Si). In this section, we describe 1- and 3-handle maps
FY,L,Si,ˆs : CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL−(Y (Si), Lσ, s′).
Let us describe the 1-handle map ﬁrst. We start with a diagram H = (Σ, α, β, w, z) such that
the image of S0 intersects Σ in two disks D1 and D2 which are in the complement of α ∪β ∪w ∪z.
We can form a diagram H′ = (Σ′, α′, β′, w, z) for (Y (S0), L) by deﬁning
Σ′ := (Σ \ (D1 ∪D2)) ∪A,
where A is an annulus, and deﬁning
α′ := α ∪{α0}
and
β′ := β ∪{β0},
where α0 and β0 are two homologically essential curves in A which intersect in exactly two points.
We will write α0 ∩β0 = {θ+, θ−}. The two points θ+ and θ−are distinguished by the Maslov
grading (note that both grw and grz induce the same decomposition of α0 ∩β0 into higher and lower
intersection points). The 1-handle map
FY,L,S0,ˆs : CFL−(H, σ, s) →CFL−(H′, σ, s′)
is deﬁned via the formula
FY,L,S0,ˆs(x) = x × θ+,

In the opposite direction, the 3-handle map for a framed 2-sphere S2 is deﬁned by picking a
Heegaard diagram H′ = (Σ′, α ∪{α0}, β ∪{β0}, w, z) such that Σ′ intersects S2 in an annulus A,
which contains both the curves α0 and β0, which are homologically essential in A, and intersect in
two points, θ+ and θ−. Furthermore A intersects no curves in α or β. We let Σ denote the surface
obtained by removing A from Σ′ and ﬁlling in the two boundary components with disks. Write
H = (Σ, α, β, w, z). If ˆs ∈Spinc(W(Y, S2)), write s and s′ for the restrictions of ˆs to Y and Y (S2),
respectively. The 3-handle map
FY,L,S2,ˆs : CFL−(H′, σ, s) →CFL−(H, σ, s′)
is deﬁned via the formula
FY,L,S2,ˆs(x × θ+) = 0
and
FY,L,S2,ˆs(x × θ−) = x,
extended equivariantly over R−
P .
Proposition 5.2. The 1-handle and 3-handle maps are chain maps for almost complex structures
which have been suﬃciently stretched on the two boundary components of the annulus A. Further-
more, the 1- and 3-handle maps are well-deﬁned in the sense that they commute with the change of
diagrams maps, up to ﬁltered, equivariant chain homotopy.
Proposition 5.2 can be proven by adapting the proofs from [Zem15, Section 8], which carry over to
the setting of link Floer homology without major change. We will state an important holomorphic
triangle map computation which is proven in [Zem15] and used to prove well-deﬁnedness of the
1-handle and 3-handle maps. If T = (Σ, α, β, γ, w, z) is Heegaard triple with two points p1, p2 ∈
Σ\(α∪β∪γ∪w∪z) we construct a new Heegaard triple T + = (Σ′, α∪{α0}, β∪{β0}, γ∪{γ0}, w, z),
as follows. We remove neighborhoods of the two points p1 and p2, and connect the resulting boundary
components with an annulus A to form the surface Σ′. In the annulus A, we add three homologically
essential curves α0, β0 and γ0. We assume that the intersection of subpair of {α0, β0, γ0} consists of
exactly two points. We will write (abusing notation slightly)
ξ0 ∩ζ0 = {θ+, θ−}
whenever ξ0 and ζ0 are distinct elements of {α0, β0, γ0}. The diagram T + is shown in Figure 5.1.

We now show that the 1-handle and 3-handle maps commute with each other. We note that if S
and S′ are disjoint framed spheres in Y , then there are diﬀeomorphisms
W(Y (S), S′) ◦W(Y, S) ∼= W(Y, S ∪S′) ∼= W(Y (S), S′) ◦W(Y, S),
which are well deﬁned up to isotopy (see Remark 10.2).
Lemma 5.4. Suppose that S and S′ are two framed spheres, which are pairwise disjoint, and which
are each either 0-dimensional or 2-dimensional. If s ∈Spinc(W(Y, S, S′)), then
FY (S),L,S′,s|W (Y (S),S′)FY,L,S,s|W (Y,S) ≃FY (S′),L,S,s|W (Y (S′),S)FY,L,S′,s|W (Y,S′).
Proof. The formulas for the maps look like they commute, though one needs to pay attention to the
almost complex structures used to compute the two compositions, since it is not a priori obvious
that a single almost complex structure can be chosen to compute both. Our argument will be similar
to the proof of Lemma 4.20. We will show the result in the case that S and S′ are both framed
0-spheres, since this is the simplest case notationally. The claim when one or both of S and S′ is a
2-sphere follows from the small adaptation of the argument we present.

φ#φ0#φ′

By transversality, since u has Maslov index 0, it must represent the constant homology class. It
is straightforward to see that this implies that φ#φ0#φ′

the other hand, the constant homology class always has a unique bJi-holomorphic representative.
Equation (13) follows, and hence the 1-handle maps for S and S′ commute.

5.4. 2-handle maps. Suppose L = (L, w, z) is a multi-based link in Y and S1 is a framed 1-
dimensional link in Y \ L. We now describe maps FY,L,S1,s associated to a framed link S1 and Spinc
structure s ∈Spinc(W(Y, S1)).
Let Lα denote the closure of the components of L \ (w ∪z) which are oriented to start in w and
end in z. Let Lβ denote the closure of the components of L \ (z ∪w) which start in z and end in w.
Deﬁnition 5.5. An α-bouquet, Bα, for a framed link S1 in Y \L is a collection of arcs which connect
each of the components of S1 to one of the components of Lα. We assume that there is exactly one
arc per component of S1, and each arc has one endpoint on S1 and one endpoint in Lα. A β-bouquet,
Bβ, for S1 is deﬁned analogously, but with endpoints on Lβ instead.
Given a bouquet B for a framed link S1 ⊆Y \L, we construct the sutured manifold Y (B) obtained
by removing a regular neighborhood of L ∪B, and adding sutures corresponding to the basepoints
w and z.
Adapting [OS06, Deﬁnition 4.2] and [Juh16, Deﬁnition 6.3], we make the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 5.6. Suppose L = (L, w, z) is a multi-based link in Y and S1 is a framed 1-dimensional
link in Y \ L. We say that a triple (Σ, α1, . . . , αn, β1, . . . , βn, β′
1, . . . , β′
n, w, z) is subordinate to a
β-bouquet Bβ for S1 if the following are satisﬁed:

Proposition 5.7. The map F β

Proposition 5.7 can be proven by adapting the proof of [OS06, Theorem 4.4] (see also [Juh16,
Theorem 6.9]), which carries over to this setting without major change.
We note that one slight diﬀerence to the argument from [OS06] is that we are working on the level of
chain complexes, instead of the level of homology. The element Θ+
β,β′ ∈HFL−(Σ, β, β′, w, z, σ, s0) is
only a well-deﬁned homology class, not a well-deﬁned cycle in the chain complex CFL−(Σ, β, β′, w, z, σ, s0).
Nonetheless, adding a boundary in CFL−(Σ, β, β′, w, z, σ, s0) to Θ+
β,β′ only changes the 2-handle
map in Equation (15) by a ﬁltered, equivariant chain homotopy, by the associativity relations for
the holomorphic triangle maps.
By adapting Deﬁnition 5.6, one deﬁnes what it means for a triple (Σ, α′, α, β, w, z) to be subor-
dinate to an α-bouquet Bα. For such a triple, the homology group HFL−(Σ, α′, α, w, z, σ, s0) will
have a distinguished element Θ+
α′,α and we deﬁne the α-subordinate 2-handle map via the formula
F α

α′,α ⊗x).
As with the case of the α-subordinate 2-handle maps, the argument from [OS06, Theorem 4.4] goes
through without major modiﬁcation to show that F α
Y,L,S1,s is independent of the choice of α-bouquet
and triple subordinate to it.
Remark 5.8. Both the α- and β-subordinate 2-handle maps count triangles such that sw = s. This
is an inherent asymmetry of the 2-handle maps between the w and z-basepoints. This asymmetry
leads to the Alexander grading change formula in [Zem17b].
We now wish to show that F α
Y,L,S1,s and F β
Y,L,S1,s are chain homotopic. The proof given by Ozsv´ath
and Szab´o in [OS06, Lemma 5.2] for the standard cobordism maps carries over without major change.
We state it as the lemma and outline their argument brieﬂy:

Lemma 5.9. If S is a framed 1-dimensional link in Y \ L and s ∈Spinc(W(Y, S)), then the maps
F α
Y,L,S,s and F β
Y,L,S,s are chain homotopic on the level of transitive systems of chain complexes.
Proof sketch. Let S′ be an isotopic copy of S, in the direction of the framing of S. One ﬁnds a
Heegaard quadruple (Σ, α′, α, β, β′, w, z) such that (Σ, α′, α, β, w, z) is triple subordinate to an
α-bouquet of S′, and (Σ, α, β, β′, w, z) is a triple subordinate to a β-bouquet of S. After surgering
Y along S, the framed link S′ ⊆Y (S) becomes a collection of 0-framed unknots. Similarly after
surgering Y on S′, the link S ⊆Y (S′) also becomes a collection of 0-framed unknots. The associativity
relations shows that,
F α
Y (S),L,S′,s0F β
Y,L,S,s ≃F β
Y (S′),L,S,s0F α
Y,L,S′,s.
Here we are writing s for the original Spinc structure on W(Y, S) and the corresponding Spinc
structure on W(Y, S′). Similarly we are writing s0 for the Spinc structures on W(Y (S), S′) and
W(Y (S′), S) which extends over the 3-handles which cancel S′ or S (resp.). The second map in each
of the compositions can be canceled by a composition of 3-handle maps, implying that F β
Y,L,S,s and
F α
Y,L,S′,s are related by post-composition by the diﬀeomorphism map associated to cancellations of
4-dimensional handles.
□
Since the triangle maps are graded over 4-dimensional Spinc structures, and there is not a canon-
ical way to compose Spinc structures, they instead satisfy a Spinc composition law. We have the
following:
Proposition 5.10. If S and S′ are two disjoint framed 1-dimensional links in Y \ L, then
FY (S),L,S′,s2FY,L,S,s1 ≃

6. Maps for band surgery
In this section, we construct maps associated to performing band surgery on a link in a 3-manifold.
A band B for a link L is an embedded copy of [−1, 1]×[−1, 1] in Y such that B∩L = {−1, 1}×[−1, 1].
In terms of link cobordisms, a band corresponds to a saddle cobordism with underlying 4-manifold
[0, 1] × Y . The surgered link L(B) is deﬁned as the union
L(B) := (L \ B) ∪([−1, 1] × {−1, 1}).
If L is an oriented link, an oriented band B is a band such that boundary orientation on ∂B is
the opposite of the orientation of L on B ∩L. If L is an oriented link and B is an oriented band for
L, then L(B) has a natural orientation induced by L. We will assume all bands are oriented.
In order to deﬁne the maps, we need to assume that the ends of the bands are disjoint from the
basepoints, and satisfy one of two conﬁgurations with respect to the basepoints. We will refer to
bands which have one of these two favorable conﬁgurations as α-bands or β-bands (see Deﬁnition 6.1).
If B has either of these conﬁgurations, then we can deﬁne
L(B) := (L(B), w, z),
which is a valid multi-based link in the sense of Deﬁnition 2.1.
If B is either an α-band or a β-band, we will deﬁne two maps
F w
B , F z
B : CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL−(Y, Lσ(B), s).
The maps F w
B and F z
B correspond to decorated link cobordisms of the form ([0, 1] × Y, F) where F
is one of the saddle cobordisms shown in Figure 6.1.
As one might expect, there are some requirements on the coloring σ which ensure that the band
maps are well-deﬁned chain maps. Analogously to the quasi-stabilization maps, the requirement on
the coloring corresponds nicely with the dividing set on the decorated link cobordism we associate
to the band maps.


===PDF PAGE 44===

We note that F w

hand, there is a simple relation between the maps for α-bands and the maps for the β-bands, which
we prove in Section 9.4.
Finally, we remark that the maps in this section use the same Heegaard triples as the maps from
[AE16].
6.1. Heegaard triples and bands. In order to deﬁne maps for saddles, we need to restrict to
bands which satisfy one of the following conditions:
Deﬁnition 6.1. We say that a band Bα of an oriented multi-based link L = (L, w, z) is an α-band
if the ends of Bα occur in components of L \ (w ∪z) which go from w-basepoints to z-basepoints.
We say that a band Bβ is a β-band if the ends of Bβ occur in components of L \ (w ∪z) going from
z-basepoints to w-basepoints. For a band B of either type, we always assume that the ends of B lie
in distinct components of L \ (w ∪z).
If Bα is an α-band for L, then we can turn Y \ (N(L ∪Bα)) into a sutured manifold by adding a
meridional suture for each basepoint of w ∪z. Write L0 for the two components of L\(w ∪z) which
contain an end of B. We deﬁne the subset H ⊆N(L ∪Bα) to be points in N(L ∪Bα) which live
over the subset L0 ∪Bα. In particular ∂H ∩N(L ∪Bα) is a 4-punctured sphere, and each puncture
of S corresponds to one of the basepoints adjacent to an end of Bα.
Deﬁnition 6.2. If Bα is an α-band for L = (L, w, z), we say that the Heegaard triple
(Σ, α′
1, . . . , α′
n, α1, . . . , αn, β1, . . . , βn, w, z)
is subordinate to Bα if the following hold:
(1) After removing neighborhoods of the w and z-basepoints from the diagram
(Σ, α1, . . . , αn−1, β1, . . . , βn, w, z)
we obtain a sutured Heegaard diagram for the sutured manifold Y \ N(L ∪Bα) (with
meridional sutures induced by the basepoints).
(2) α′
1, . . . , α′
n−1 are small Hamiltonian isotopies of the curves α1, . . . , αn−1 with |α′
i∩αj| = 2δij,
for i, j ∈{1, . . . , n −1}, where δij denotes the Kronecker delta.
(3) Let L0, S and H be as deﬁned in the previous paragraph. Let A ⊆S be the closed curve
which is speciﬁed up to isotopy by the property that A bounds a disk in H which separates
the two components of L0, and let A′ ⊆S denote the closed curve which is speciﬁed up to
isotopy by the property that A′ bounds a disk in H which separates the two components of
L0(B). Then αn is a curve which is obtained by projecting A onto Σ \ (α1 ∪· · · ∪αn−1) and
α′
n is the curve which is obtained by projecting A′ onto Σ \ (α′

It is well known that any two sutured Heegaard diagrams for a sutured manifold can be connected
by a simple set of Heegaard moves [JT12, Proposition 2.37].
Applying this fact to the sutured
manifold Y \ N(L ∪Bα), we obtain the following:
Lemma 6.3. Given an α-band Bα, any two triples subordinate to Bα can be connected by a sequence
of the following moves
(1) Isotopies Σt of the Heegaard surface inside of Y such that Σt ∩(L ∪B) = w ∪z for all t.
(2) Isotopies or handleslides amongst the β-curves.
(3) Isotopies or handleslides amongst the α1, . . . , αn−1 curves followed by the corresponding
handleslide or isotopy of the corresponding curve in α′
1, . . . , α′
n−1.
(4) Isotopies or handleslides of the curve αn across other α-curves.
(5) Isotopies or handleslides of the curve α′
n across other α′-curves.
(6) Stabilizations of the Heegaard triple, i.e., taking the internal connected sum of Σ in Y with
a torus T2 ⊆Y such that there is a 3-ball B3 ⊆Y containing T2 with B3 ∩(Σ∪L∪B) = ∅.
On T2 we put three curves, α′
0, α0 and β0, with |α0 ∩β0| = 1 and α′
0 a small Hamiltonian
isotopy of α0 with |α′
0 ∩α0| = 2.
Deﬁnition 6.2 can be easily modiﬁed to provide a deﬁnition of a Heegaard triple (Σ, α, β, β′) being
subordinate to a β-band. Similarly, Lemma 6.3 can be easily modiﬁed to give a set of moves between
any two Heegaard triples which are subordinate to a β-band.
If (Σ, α′, α, β, w, z) is a Heegaard triple subordinate to a band, we will deﬁne the band maps
by counting holomorphic triangles. To do this, it is important to understand the chain complex
CFL−(Σ, α′, α, w, z). To this end, we prove the following:
Lemma 6.4. If (Σ, α′, α, β, w, z) is a Heegaard triple subordinate to an α-band Bα ⊆Y , then
(Σ, α′, α, w, z) is a diagram for an unlink in (a disjoint union of copies of) (S1 × S2)#g(Σ), and
every component of the unlink has exactly two basepoints, except one component, which has four.
Proof. Let (Yα′,α, Lα′,α) denote the 3-manifold and link induced by the diagram (Σ, α′, α, w, z).
Write Lα′,α for the underlying link of Lα′,α. Note that for i ∈{1, . . . , n −1} the pair (α′
i, αi)
determines an embedded 2-sphere Si in Yα′,α which does not intersect Lα′,α. By surgering out Si,
we can reduce to the case that Σ consists of |Lα′,α| genus 0 components. There are |Lα′,α| −1 of
these components which have no α′ or α curves, but have a single pair of w and z-basepoints, thus
determining an unknotted component of Lα′,α in Yα′,α with exactly two basepoints. There will be
one component of the resulting Heegaard diagram which contains the curves α′
n and αn. A model

□
