Lemma 6.5. The map F z
B is well-deﬁned up to equivariant, ﬁltered chain homotopy.
Proof. We just need to check independence from each of the moves appearing Lemma 6.3. Each of
these moves is considered in the maps associated to surgery on framed 1-dimensional links embedded
in 3-manifolds, and can be addressed using the same argument as [OS06, Proposition 4.6].
□
We also make the following remark:
Remark 6.6. Since the element Θw is well-deﬁned on the level of homology, it is not necessary to
work with Heegaard triples which satisfy Deﬁnition 6.2 exactly as it is stated. Rather it is suﬃcient
to work with triples which are related to the ones described in Deﬁnition 6.2 by a sequence of
handleslides and isotopies of the attaching curves.

7. Two compound handle attachment maps
In this section, we describe two compound handle maps, which we deﬁne as compositions of the
maps from Sections 5 and 6. The ﬁrst type of compound map are the birth and death cobordism
maps, which will be useful later when we prove several facts about the band maps (notably Propo-
sition 9.10). The next map we deﬁne is the compound 1-handle/band map, for a framed 0-sphere
along a link L, which will be useful later when we prove invariance of the cobordism maps.
7.1. Birth/death cobordism maps. Suppose that L = (L, w, z) is a multi-based link in Y 3 and
U = (U, w, z) is an doubly based unknot in Y , and D is a choice of embedded disk in Y \ L which
has boundary U. Write L ∪U for the link (L ∪U, w ∪{w}, z ∪{z}). Suppose σ and σ′ are colorings
of L and L ∪U (resp.) such that σ′ restricts to σ. We deﬁne maps
B+
U,D : CFL◦(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL◦(Y, (L ∪U)σ′, s)
and
D−
U,D : CFL◦(Y, (L ∪U)σ′, s) →CFL◦(Y, Lσ, s)
which are well-deﬁned on the level of transitive chain homotopy types. We will describe the maps
on the level of intersection points, and state the eﬀect of adding a doubly based unknot on the
chain complex CFL−. We will not explicitly prove invariance of the maps B+
U,D and D−
U,D, since our
deﬁnition of the map B+

adds the unknot U inside of a disjoint copy of S3) as well as a 1-handle map. Similarly D−

written as a composition of a 3-handle map and a 4-handle map. Invariance of the maps B+
U,D and
D−
U,D hence follows from invariance of the 0-, 1-, 3- and 4-handle maps, which we proved earlier.
We pick a regular neighborhood N(D) of D which does not intersect L and consider only diagrams

(2) N(D) ∩(α ∪β ∪w ∪z) = ∅.

7.2. Surgeries and traces of framed 0-spheres along L. It will be convenient for our proof
of invariance to consider framed 0-spheres S0 which are centered at a pair of points contained in
the link L.
In this section, we describe traces and surgeries of such framed 0-spheres.
In the
following section, we will deﬁne cobordism maps associated to framed 0-spheres along L. We make
the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 7.3. Suppose L is an oriented link in Y . We say that a smooth embedding S0 : S0×D3 ,→
Y is a framed 0-sphere along L if the following hold:
(1) If we write D3 as {(y, w, z) : y2+w2+z2 ≤1}, then (S0)−1(L) = S0×{(y, 0, 0) : −1 ≤y ≤1}.
(2) Each of S0(S0 × {(1, 0, 0)}) and S0(S0 × {(−1, 0, 0)}) contains an incoming point of L, and
an outgoing point of L, according to the orientation of L.
We now describe the surgeries and traces of a framed 0-sphere which occurs along L, extending the
notation from Section 5.1. If S0 is a framed 0-sphere along L ⊆Y , we deﬁne a link L(S0) ⊆Y (S0)
via the equation
L(S0) := (L \ int im(S0)) ∪([−1, 1] × {±1, 0, 0}).
Condition (1) of Deﬁnition 7.3 implies that the link L(S0) is a closed 1-manifold in Y (S0), while
Condition (2) implies that an orientation of L uniquely determines an orientation on L(S0).
We now deﬁne the trace of S0, as well as the induced trace link cobordism. It is convenient to
deﬁne it slightly diﬀerently than in Section 5.1. We deﬁne the 4-manifold
W(Y, S0) := ([0, 1] × Y ) ∪(D1 × D3) ∪([1, 2] × Y (S0)).
There is an oriented surface Σ(L, S0) ⊆W(Y, S0) deﬁned as
Σ(L, S0) := ([0, 1] × L) ∪B ∪([1, 2] × L(S0))
where B ⊆[−1, 1] × D3 is the subset B := [−1, 1] × {(y, w, z) : −1 ≤y ≤1, z = w = 0}. Note that
B is canonically identiﬁed with [−1, 1] × [−1, 1].
We now deﬁne the trace link cobordism of S0 to be
W(Y, L, S0) := (W(Y, S0), Σ(L, S0)): (Y, L) →(Y (S0), L(S0)).
7.3. Construction of the maps for framed 0-spheres along L. Analogously to the situation
of the band maps, to deﬁne maps for framed 0-spheres along L, we will need to restrict to framed
0-spheres which satisfy several additional requirements:
Deﬁnition 7.4. Suppose S0 is a framed 0-sphere along L. We say that S0 is of α-type if the two
components of im(S0) are contained in distinct α-subarcs of L \ (w ∪z) (i.e. they are contained in
two distinct components of Uα ∩(L \ (w ∪z))). We say that S0 is of β-type if the two components
of im(S0) are contained in two distinct β-subarcs of L \ (w ∪z).
Under the above assumptions, L(S0) := (L(S0), w, z) is a valid multi-based link. If s ∈Spinc(Y ),
then s admits a unique extension to W(Y, S0), which we denote by ˆs. We let s′ denote the restriction
of ˆs to Y (S0). A coloring σ of L, induces a coloring of L(S0), for which we also write σ. For a framed
0-sphere which is of either α-type or β-type, we will deﬁne a type-z compound 1-handle/band map
F z
Y,L,S0,ˆs : CFL−(Y, Lσ, s) →CFL−(Y (S0), (L(S0))σ, s′),

Lemma 7.5. Suppose S0 is a framed zero sphere along L which is of α-type or β-type and o ∈{w, z}.

Proof. Fix o ∈{w, z}. The extended half disks Hi depend on a contractible set of choices (a vector
ﬁeld pointing out of the boundary of S0, and a Riemannian metric), and hence it is easy to see that
the maps do not depend on the extensions Hi.

constant on S0. Note that since Ψ is constant on S0, there is an induced self-diﬀeomorphism ΨS0 of
Y (S0).

8. Basic relations involving the handle maps, band maps and quasi-stabilization
maps
In this section, we prove some basic relations involving the handle attachment maps, the band
maps, and the quasi-stabilization maps.
8.1. Basic relations between handle attachments, quasi-stabilizations and basepoint
actions. We ﬁrst show that the 1-handle and 3-handle maps always commute with the quasi-
stabilization maps:
Lemma 8.1. Suppose L is a multi-based link in Y , and L+

almost complex structure can be chosen to compute both compositions.

8.2. Quasi-stabilizations and critical point cancellations. In this section, we show that the
quasi-stabilization maps, birth and death cobordism maps, and band maps satisfy a set of relations
which we can interpret as showing invariance of the link cobordism maps under index 1/2 critical
point cancellations of the surface a the link cobordism. Notice that if we ignore the dividing set,
a birth cobordism adds a disk to the surface of a link cobordism.
The birth cobordism can be
canceled by attaching a band, leaving a link cobordism which is diﬀeomorphic to the identity link
cobordism, as an undecorated link cobordism. However as a decorated link cobordism, the resulting
link cobordism will not be the identity. Instead, we are left with the dividing set corresponding to
a quasi-stabilization map. The manipulation is shown in Figure 8.2.

for the small triangle shown on the top of Figure 8.3. The formula is preserved when we splice in
a class on (S2, α′
0, α0, β0) in any of the π2(t, t), and it is easy to see that on this diagram, any two
homology classes with the same endpoints can be related by a sum of such classes.
Combining Equations (29) and (31), we obtain
(32)
⟨FT +,s,Js(T )(x × θw, y × θ+), θw⟩= FT ,s,Js(x, y)
Similarly, if ψ0 ∈π2(θw, θ−, θz) is a homology class of triangles, then
(33)
m(ψ0) + nw(ψ0) + 1 = nz(ψ0) + nz′(ψ0).
As before, this can be veriﬁed by checking it for a single class (for example the class shown on the
bottom of Figure 8.3), and then checking that the formula respects splicing in a class of disks in any
π2(t, t). Combining Equations (30) and (33) we obtain the equality:
(34)
⟨FT +,s,Js(T )(x × θw, y × θ−), ξw⟩= V · FT ,s,Js(x, y).
Combining Equations (28), (32), and (34), we obtain the lemma statement.

9. Further relations involving the band maps
In this section, we prove some important properties of the band maps.
9.1. Band maps and basepoint actions. In this section, we describe when the maps Φw, and
Ψz commute with the band maps F z
B and F w
B :
Lemma 9.1. Suppose B is a band for L = (L, w, z), of type-α or -β, and w ∈w and z ∈z are
basepoints. Then
(1) F z
BΦw + ΦwF z
B ≃0, regardless of the position of w,
(2) F z
BΨz + ΨzF z
B ≃0, if z is not adjacent to an end of B.
Proof. Let us assume for concreteness that B is an α-band. Write z1 and z2 for the two z-basepoints
adjacent to the ends of B. Write P0 = (w ∪z)/(z1 ∼z2) and let σ0 : w ∪z →P0 be the natural
map. Let (Σ, α′, α, β, w, z) be a triple subordinate to B. By deﬁnition, the band map
F z
B : CFL−(Σ, α, β, w, z, σ0, s) →CFL−(Σ, α′, β, w, z, σ0, s)
is deﬁned by the formula
F z
B(−) := Fα′,α,β,s(Θw, −).
Gromov compactness combined with the fact that Θw is a cycle on the complex which is colored by
σ0 implies that
(35)
F z
B∂α,β = ∂α′,βF z
B.
If ∂denotes the diﬀerential on either CFL−(Σ, α′, β, σ0) or CFL−(Σ, α, β, σ0), we have
d
dUw
(∂) = Φw
and
d
dVz
(∂) = Ψz,
as long as z ̸∈{z1, z2}. Hence, diﬀerentiating Equation (35) and using the Leibniz rule yields
F z
BΦw ≃ΦwF z
B
and
F z
BΨz ≃ΨzF z
B,
as long as z ̸∈{z1, z2}.
Finally we note that these relations persist for an arbitrary coloring σ: w ∪z →P such that
σ(z1) = σ(z2), by tensoring.
□

Lemma 9.4. Suppose that U = (U, w, z) is an unlink in (S1 × S2)#k such that all components of
U have 2 basepoints, except one which has 4. Write w1, w2, z1 and z2 for the basepoints on the

Proof. We ﬁrst check the claim for an unknot U0 with four basepoints in S3. In that case, we can take
a diagram (S2, α0, β0, w1, w2, z1, z2) where α0 and β0 intersect at two points, and S2\(α0∪β0) consists
of four bigons, each containing a single basepoint. In this case, the claim is an easy computation,
since Φwi counts only the bigon going over wi, and similarly Ψzi counts the bigon going over zi. Let
us write Θw

0, s0).
We now verify the claim for an arbitrary unlink U in (S1 × S2)#k, all of whose link components

cobordism from (S3, U0) to ((S1 × S2)#k, U) consisting of |U| −1 0-handles (which each add a
doubly based unknot) and k 1-handles. Write F for the composition of the corresponding 0-handle
and 1-handle maps. By inspection we have
(36)
F(Θw

and
F(Θz

0 ) = Θw
0 .
A similar argument shows that Ψzi(Θw) = Θz.

9.2. Band maps and quasi-stabilization. We now consider the commutator between a band
map and a quasi-stabilization map. One approach would be to simply try to perform a holomorphic
degeneration argument, as in Proposition 4.28. Unfortunately the result stated therein is not suﬃ-
cient to show that the band maps commute with the quasi-stabilization maps in all of the cases that
we are interested in. It is possible to compute the commutator of a quasi-stabilization and a band
map by adapting [Zem17c, Theorem 6.5], however we will instead give a simpler, though somewhat
less direct argument.

9.3. Commutation of band maps I. In this subsection, we prove that band maps can always be
commuted, as long as one is an α-band, and the other is a β-band. We will later consider commuting

9.4. Band maps and basepoint moving maps. We now consider the relation between α-bands
and β-bands. Recall that we call a band an α-band if the two ends of the band are in components
of L \ (w ∪z) which go from w to z. Similarly, we say a band is a β-band if the two ends of the
band lie in components of L \ (w ∪z) which go from z to w.
We claimed earlier that the distinction between α- and β-bands was not especially important for
the TQFT structure, in terms of which decorated link cobordism the maps represented. Instead, we
claimed that the more important distinction is between the w- and z-versions of the band maps. In
this section, we make that claim precise.


===PDF PAGE 64===

across z1 and z2. There is a diﬀeomorphism
φ: (Y, L(Bβ)) →(Y, L(Bα))

Remark 9.11. Proposition 9.10 can be interpreted similarly to [OS06, Lemma 5.2], which says that
one can compute maps induced by surgery on framed links using either left subordinate or right sub-

ordinate maps, for 0-surgery on an unlink. Our proof of Proposition 9.10 follows from the analogous
observation that the birth cobordism map B+

a β-band map.

10. Parametrized Kirby decompositions of link cobordisms
In this section, we deﬁne a way of decomposing a link cobordism into elementary pieces, which
we call a parametrized Kirby decomposition, borrowing the terminology of [Juh16].
10.1. Parametrized link cobordisms.
Deﬁnition 10.1. If (W, Σ): (Y, L) →(Y ′, L′) is an undecorated link cobordism, a parametrization
of (W, Σ), is a collection of pairwise disjoint framed spheres S in Y , together with a diﬀeomorphism
of link cobordisms
Φ: W(Y, L, S) →(W, Σ).
such that Φ|{0}×Y = idY . Furthermore, we assume S is either a collection of framed spheres in
Y \ L, or is a single framed 0-sphere along L. In the above expression, W(Y, L, S) denotes the trace
link cobordism obtained by attaching 4-dimensional handles along S to Y \ L (Section 5.1), or a
attaching a 4-dimensional 1-handle containing a 2-dimensional 1-handle if S is a framed 0-sphere
along L (Section 7.2).
The following basic fact will be useful:
Remark 10.2. Suppose S and S′ are disjoint collections of framed spheres in Y , and assume for nota-
tional convenience that S and S′ are in the complement of L. Then the link cobordisms W(Y, L, S∪S′),
W(Y (S), L, S′) ◦W(Y, L, S), and W(Y (S′), L, S) ◦W(Y, L, S′) are all diﬀeomorphic. The diﬀeomor-
phism is canonically speciﬁed up to isotopy ﬁxing {0} × Y , and preserving Σ. To see this, it is
suﬃcient to specify the diﬀeomorphism from the W(Y, L, S ∪S′) to W(Y (S), L, S′) ◦W(Y, L, S).
Notice that we can think of the second as being equal to the ﬁrst with an extra copy of [0, 1] × Y (S)
inserted. A choice of bicollar neighborhood of {0}×Y (S) allows us to specify such a diﬀeomorphism,
up to isotopy. Since any two bicollar neighborhoods are isotopic, a diﬀeomorphism between the two
cobordisms is speciﬁed uniquely up to isotopy ﬁxing {0} × Y and preserving Σ.
Now suppose (W, Σ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) and (W ′, Σ′): (Y2, L2) →(Y3, L3) are two link cobor-
disms with parametrizations
Φ: W(Y1, L1, S1) →(W, Σ),
and
Φ′ : W(Y2, L2, S2) →(W ′, Σ′).
For notational simplicity, assume that no components of S1 or S2 are framed 0-spheres along L1
or L2. Let S′
2 ⊆Y1(S1) denote Φ|−1
Y1(S1)(S2). The diﬀeomorphism Φ|Y1(S2) : Y (S2) →Y2 induces a
diﬀeomorphism eΦ: W(Y1(S1), S′
2) →W(Y2, S2).
We deﬁne the concatenation of the two parametrizations
Φ′ ∗Φ: W(Y1(S1), L1, S′
2) ◦W(Y1, L1, S1) →(W ′, Σ′) ◦(W, Σ),
by setting Φ′ ∗Φ to be Φ on W(Y1, L1, S1) and Φ′ ◦eΦ on W(Y1(S1), L1, S′
2). If S′
2 ⊆Y1 \ S1, then in
light of Remark 10.2, the concatenated diﬀeomorphisms induce a parametrization
Φ′ ∗Φ: W(Y1, L1, S1 ∪S′
2) →(W ′, Σ′) ◦(W, Σ)
which is well-deﬁned up to isotopies (of link cobordisms) ﬁxing Y1 × {0}. The concatenation of the
two parametrizations can also be deﬁned in the case that S1 or S2 is a framed link along L1 or L2,
with only the obvious notational changes to the domain of Φ′ ∗Φ.
Analogous to [Juh15], we need a notion of elementary parametrized cobordisms, corresponding to
link cobordisms which have a Morse function with at most one critical value of f|W \Σ, f|Σ or f|A:
Deﬁnition 10.3. A tuple W = (W, Fσ, Φ, S) is called an elementary, parametrized link cobordism
if (W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is a decorated link cobordism with ∂W = −Y1 ⊔Y2, F = (Σ, A) is
a surface with divides, and S is a (possibly empty) collection of framed spheres (0-, 1-, or 2-spheres
away from L, or 0-spheres along L) in Y1 which are pairwise disjoint. Furthermore, we assume that
exactly one of the following holds:
(EPC-1): S = S∅and Φ: W(Y1, L1, S∅) →(W, Σ) is a parametrization which is the identity
on Y1 × {0} and is deﬁned up to isotopies of link cobordisms which ﬁx Y1 × {0}.
Furthermore, each arc of A goes from the incoming boundary to the outgoing boundary.


===PDF PAGE 69===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
69
(EPC-2): S = S∅and Φ is a parametrization
Φ: W(Y1, L1, S∅) →(W, Σ),
which is the identity on Y1×{0}, and is deﬁned up to isotopies ﬁxing Y1×{0}. Further-
more, all divides of A, except for exactly one arc A, go from the incoming boundary to
the outgoing boundary. There are four subtypes:
(EPC-2S+): The arc A has two ends on the outgoing boundary, and the arc bounds a bigon of
type-w.
(EPC-2S−): The arc A has two ends on the incoming boundary, and the arc bounds a bigon of
type-w.
(EPC-2T +): The arc A has two ends on the outgoing boundary, and the arc bounds a bigon of
type-z.
(EPC-2T −): The arc A has two ends on the incoming boundary, and the arc bounds a bigon of
type-z.
(EPC-3): S is a framed 0-sphere, embedded along L1, and Φ is a parametrization
Φ: W(Y1, L1, S) →(W, Σ),
which is the identity on Y1 × {0}, and such that the dividing set Φ−1(A) ⊆Σ(L1, S) is
disjoint from B ⊆Σ(L1, S). The parametrization is well-deﬁned up to isotopies which
ﬁx Y1 × {0} and the point (0, 0) ∈B. Furthermore, all of the arcs in A go from the
incoming boundary to the outgoing boundary.
The type (w or z) of each region of Σ(L1, S) \ Φ−1(A) is well-deﬁned. Hence there
are two subtypes:
(EPC-3w): The region containing the band is a component of Σw.
(EPC-3z): The region containing the band is a component of Σz.
(EPC-4): S ̸= S∅and S is a framed 0-sphere, 1-dimensional link or 2-sphere in Y1 \ L1 and Φ is
a parametrization
Φ: W(Y1, L1, S) →(W, Σ)
which is the identity on Y1×{0}, and which is deﬁned up to isotopies of link cobordisms
which ﬁx Y1 × {0}. Furthermore, each arc of the divides A goes from the incoming
boundary to the outgoing boundary.
The dividing sets for the 4-subtypes of (EPC-3) cobordisms are illustrated in Figure 12.1.
Remark 10.4. In Deﬁnition (10.3), the diﬀeomorphism Φ is of undecorated link cobordisms, and does
not necessarily respect the divides beyond what is stated in (EPC-3) link cobordisms. Furthermore,
for elementary parametrized cobordisms of type (EPC-1), (EPC-2) and (EPC-4), the parametrization
Φ is only deﬁned up to isotopies which ﬁx Y1 × {0}. For elementary parametrized cobordisms of
type (EPC-3), it is deﬁned up to isotopies which ﬁx Y1 × {0} and the point (0, 0) ∈B.
We make the following deﬁnition, borrowing the terminology from [Juh16] and [Juh15]:
Deﬁnition 10.5. If (W, F): (Y, L) →(Y ′, L′) is a decorated link cobordism, we deﬁne a parametrized
Kirby decomposition K of (W, F) to be the following collection of data:
(1) A decomposition of (W, F) as
(W, F) = (Wn, Fn) ∪Yn · · · ∪Y1 (W0, F0)
where (Wi, Fi) is a decorated link cobordism with ∂Wi = −Yi ⊔Yi+1 and ∂Fi = −Li ⊔Li+1,
for 3-manifolds with links (Yi, Li) and surface with divides Fi (all embedded in W), such that
Y0 = Y and Yn+1 = Y ′. Furthermore A intersects each component of each Li non-trivially
and transversally.
(2) A choice of basepoints wi and zi on each Li, with one basepoint per component of Li \ A,
of the same type as the subsurface of Σ \ A which contains it.
(3) A framed sphere or link, Si ⊆Yi and a parameterizing diﬀeomorphism Φi, making each
(Wi, Fi, Φi, Si) an elementary, parametrized link cobordism.


===PDF PAGE 70===
70
IAN ZEMKE
We assume further that there is only one term of K of type (EPC-4) which contains a 1-dimensional
framed link. Additionally, for convenience, we assume that all terms of the decomposition of type
(EPC-4) occur after all of the terms of type (EPC-2) or (EPC-3), and that terms of the decomposition
of type (EPC-4) appear with non-decreasing index (i.e. the 0-spheres occur before the 1-dimensional
links, which occur before the 2-spheres).
11. Moves between parametrized Kirby decompositions
In this section, we deﬁne a notion of equivalence between two parametrized Kirby decompositions,
which we call Cerf equivalence. The main theorem of this section is Theorem 11.5, which states
that any two parametrized Kirby decompositions of a decorated link cobordism (W, F) are Cerf
equivalent. Most of this section is devoted to proving Theorem 11.5.
11.1. Cerf equivalences of parametrized Kirby decompositions. In this section we provide
the deﬁnition of Cerf equivalence (Deﬁnition 11.4). Before we can state the deﬁnition, we describe
several constructions which will feature in the deﬁnition.
We ﬁrst describe a special notion of isotopy between two parametrized Kirby decompositions,
which will be one of the moves. Suppose that K is a parametrized Kirby decomposition of the deco-
rated link cobordism (W, F): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2). Write F = (Σ, A) and suppose that φ: (W, Σ) →
(W, Σ) is a diﬀeomorphism which is the identity on Y1. We now describe a decomposition φ∗K of
(W, F) into parametrized cobordisms, which we note may fail to be a parametrized Kirby decompo-
sition in the sense of Deﬁnition 10.5. If
(W, Σ) = (Wn, Σn) ∪Yn · · · ∪Y1 (W0, Σ0)
is the decomposition of undecorated link cobordisms associated to K, then we deﬁne the decompo-
sition associated to φ∗K to be
(50)
(φ(Wn), φ(Σn)) ∪φ(Yn) · · · ∪φ(Y1) (φ(W0), φ(Σ0)).
The framed spheres Si and parametrizations Φi of K can also be pushed forward along φ to give
diﬀeomorphisms
φ∗(Φi) := φ ◦Φi ◦φ−1 : W(φ(Yi), φ(Li), φ(Si)) →(φ(Wi), φ(Σi)).
Note that φ∗K may fail to be a parametrized Kirby decomposition, because in general there is no
reason to expect (φ(Wi), φ(Σi), φ(Σi)∩A) to be an elementary parametrized cobordism in the sense
of Deﬁnition 10.3. With this in mind, we make the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 11.1. Suppose that (W, F) is a link cobordism with F = (Σ, A). If K and K′ are two
parametrized Kirby decompositions of (W, F), we say that K and K′ are related by an A-adapted
isotopy if there is a 1-parameter family of diﬀeomorphisms φt : (W, Σ) →(W, Σ) such that following
are satisﬁed:
(1) φt|Y1 = id for all t.
(2) (φt)∗K is a valid parametrized Kirby decomposition for all t.
(3) (φ1)∗K = K′.
Remark 11.2. Suppose φt is an A-adapted isotopy of (W, F) and (Wi, Fi, Φi, S0
i ) is a term of K
corresponding to a framed 0-sphere along Li. Writing p for Φi(0, 0), where (0, 0) center of the band
in the cobordism W(Yi, Li, S0
i ), then φt(p) is a path on Σ. For φt to induce an A-adapted isotopy,
this path cannot intersect an arc of A, since at a point of time when φt(p) ∈A, the diﬀeomorphism
(φt)∗Φi fails to be a valid parametrization for an elementary parametrized cobordism, as it maps
the center of the band onto an arc of A.
Additionally, we need the following deﬁnition related to birth-death singularities of Morse functions
(compare [Juh15, Section 1.3]):


===PDF PAGE 71===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
71
Deﬁnition 11.3. Suppose S ⊆Y is a framed k-sphere, and S′ ⊆Y (S) is a framed (k + 1)-sphere
which intersects the belt sphere of S, transversely at a single point. We say a diﬀeomorphism
Ψ: W(Y (S), Li, S′) ◦W(Y, L, S) →W(Y, L, S∅),
is formed by a handle cancellation if it is constructed by the following procedure: take two Morse
functions and gradient-like vector ﬁelds on W(Y (S), L, S′) and W(Y, L, S) which each have exactly
one critical point, and induce the identity parametrization of the two cobordisms. In Section 11.6
we will describe exactly how a Morse function and gradient-like vector ﬁeld induce a parametrizing
diﬀeomorphism. Furthermore, we will see that such Morse functions and gradient-like vector ﬁelds
exist in Lemma 11.21. The stable manifold of the higher critical point is transverse to the unstable
manifold of the lower critical point and there is a single ﬂowline connecting the two critical points.
Write (f, v) for the Morse function and gradient-like vector ﬁeld obtained by stacking the Morse func-
tion and gradient-like vector ﬁelds on the two cobordisms. Using the argument from [Juh15, Section
1.3], there is a homotopically unique path of functions with gradient-like vector ﬁelds (ft, vt)t∈[0,1]
with f0 = f, which are constant outside a neighborhood of the ﬂowline between the two critical
points, which realizes a birth-death singularity. The pair (f1, v1) on W(Y (S), L, S′) ◦W(Y, L, S)
determines a diﬀeomorphism Ψ: W(Y, L, S∅) →W(Y (S), L, S′) ◦W(Y, L, S), which is the identity
on the incoming boundary, {0} × Y , and is well-deﬁned up to isotopy relative {0} × Y , since (f1, v1)
is determined up to homotopy (through paths of Morse functions and gradient-like vector ﬁelds,
with no intermediate singularities).
We can now give our deﬁnition of equivalence between two parametrized Kirby decompositions.

The remainder of this section is devoted to proving the following theorem, which will be used to
prove invariance of the link cobordism maps:
Theorem 11.5. If (W, F): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is a decorated link cobordism and each component
of W and each component of F intersect Y1 and Y2 non-trivially, then any two parametrized Kirby
decompositions of (W, F) are Cerf equivalent.
11.2. Morse functions and adapted coordinates. Suppose that X is a smooth manifold with a
Morse function f : X →R and p ∈X is an index k critical point of f. If U ⊆X is a precompact set,
let Coorp(U) denote the set of oriented local coordinates ⃗x = (x1, . . . , xn) deﬁned on U, centered at
p, which extend smoothly to a neighborhood of cl(U) ⊆X. We will write Coorp(U, f) for the subset
of Coorp(U) of coordinates ⃗x = (x1, . . . , xn) where
f = f(p) −(x2
1 + · · · + x2
k) + (x2
k+1 + · · · + x2
n).
The sets Coorp(U) and Coorp(U, f) can be given the C∞topology.
If X and Y are open subsets of Rn, both containing ⃗0, and X is precompact, we will write
Diﬀ′((X,⃗0), (Y,⃗0)) for the space of maps from X to Y which ﬁx ⃗0, are diﬀeomorphisms onto their
image, and which extend smoothly to a neighborhood of cl(X) in Rn.
We will write F y
k,n−k : Rn →R for the function

and only if ⃗x ∈Coorp(U, f).
The map F y

F y

where Ik,n−k is the diagonal matrix whose ﬁrst k entries are −1 and the last n −k are +1. The
indeﬁnite orthogonal group O(k, n−k) is deﬁned as the set of n×n matrices M over R which satisfy
M ⊤· Ik,n−k · M = Ik,n−k. If 0 < k < n, then O(k, n −k) has 4 components, which can be related
by reversing the orientation of one or both of the subspaces Rk × {⃗0}, {⃗0} × Rn−k ⊆Rk × Rn−k. If
0 < k < n, then SO(k, n −k), the set of matrices in O(k, n −k) with determinant +1 (equivalently

the matrices in O(k, n −k) whose action preserves the orientation of Rn), has two components. The
two components are related by reversing the orientations of Rk ×{⃗0} and {⃗0}×Rn−k simultaneously.

We now prove several reﬁnements of Lemma 11.6, concerning spaces of coordinates adapted to
Morse functions and surfaces in a 4-manifold. Suppose Σ2 is an oriented, embedded surface in a
smooth manifold X4. Suppose U ⊆X is a precompact open subset, f : X →R is a Morse function
such that f|Σ is Morse, and p ∈Σ is a critical point of f and f|Σ, which is index 1 for both.
Write Coorp(U, Σ, f) to be the set of coordinates ⃗x = (x, y, w, z) for X, which extend smoothly to a
neighborhood of cl(U), such that Σ ∩U = im(⃗x) ∩{(x, y, 0, 0)} and
f = f(p) −x2 + y2 + w2 + z2
on U.

We prove a ﬁnal result concerning Morse functions and coordinate systems. Suppose that Σ2 is
an embedded submanifold of X4 and both are oriented. Suppose that h is a Morse function on Σ,
and p ∈Σ is an index 1 critical point of h. If U ⊆X is a precompact open set containing p, let
Coorp(U, Σ, h) denote the set of coordinates ⃗x: U →R4, centered at p, which extend smoothly to a
neighborhood of cl(U), such that ⃗x(U ∩Σ) is embedded in {(w, z, 0, 0)} in an orientation preserving
manner, and such that
h = h(p) −x2 + y2
on Σ ∩U.
Lemma 11.8. Let Σ2 be an oriented submanifold of X4, and let h be a Morse function on Σ with a
critical point p of index 1. If Coorp(U, Σ, h) contains an element ⃗x0 such that ⃗x0(U) ⊆R4 is convex,
then the space Coorp(U, Σ, h) has 2 components, which are related by replacing (x, y) with (−x, −y).
Proof. The technique from Lemma 11.6 shows that if Coorp(U, Σ, h) contains such an ⃗x0, then
Coorp(U, Σ, h) is homotopy equivalent to the space of linear transformations of R4 which preserve
{(x, y, 0, 0)} setwise, preserve the orientations of R4 and {(x, y, 0, 0)}, and preserve the function
h(p) −x2 + y2 on Σ. Such a linear transformation can be written in block matrix form as
M =

0
D

where A ∈O(1, 1), B ∈Mat2×2(R) and D ∈GL(2, R).
The set of such matrices deformation
retracts onto the space with B = 0. Furthermore, requiring that M preserve the orientations of X
and Σ implies that A and D have positive determinant, so we see that Coorp(U, Σ, h) is homotopy
equivalent to SO(1, 1)×GL+(2, R), which has two components, which are related by replacing (x, y)
with (−x, −y).

Deﬁnition 11.9. If (W, Σ) is an undecorated link cobordism, we deﬁne M(W, Σ) to be the space
of Morse functions f : W →[0, 2] such that the following hold:
(1) Crit(f|Σ) = Crit(f) ∩Σ.


===PDF PAGE 77===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
77
(2) For each p ∈Crit(f) ∩Σ there are coordinates ⃗x = (x, y, w, z), deﬁned on a neighborhood
U of p, which are oriented for both W and Σ, such that f = f(p) −x2 + y2 + w2 + z2, and
⃗x(Σ ∩U) = ⃗x(U) ∩{(x, y, 0, 0)}.
If h is a ﬁxed Morse function on Σ which has only index 1 critical points, we will write M(W, Σ; h)
for the set of Morse functions in M(W, Σ) which also restrict to h on Σ.
Remark 11.10. If f ∈M(W, Σ), then by property (2) of Deﬁnition 11.9, it follows that f|Σ has only
index 1 critical points.
Deﬁnition 11.11. If (W, Σ) is a link cobordism, we say that a smooth function f : W →[0, 2] is a
nice Morse function if the following are satisﬁed:
(1) f ∈M(W, Σ).
(2) All critical values of f|Σ are in (0, 1), and all critical values of f|W \Σ are in (1, 2).
(3) f|W \Σ has only index 1, 2 or 3 critical points.
(4) All index 1 critical points of f|W \Σ occur before the index 2 critical points, which occur
before the index 3 critical points.
(5) All critical points of f have distinct critical values.
We write Mnice(W, Σ) for the set of nice Morse functions. If h is a ﬁxed Morse function on Σ, we
write Mnice(W, Σ; h) for the subset of Mnice(W, Σ) of Morse functions which restrict to h on Σ.
11.3. Connecting Morse functions which agree on Σ. In this subsection, we construct appro-
priately nice paths between Morse functions which agree on Σ.
Lemma 11.12. Suppose that h is a Morse function on Σ with only index 1 critical points.
If
f0, f1 ∈M(W, Σ, h), then there is a 1-parameter family (ft)t∈[0,1] of smooth functions such that
ft ∈M(W, Σ, h) for all but ﬁnitely many t, and at the ﬁnitely many points of time when ft fails to
be in M(W, Σ, h), exactly one of the following occurs:
(1) A critical point birth-death occurs in W \ Σ.
(2) Two critical points exchange relative value.
Furthermore the path ft can be chosen so that for each p ∈Crit(h), there is a neighborhood U of p
in W and a 1-parameter family of coordinates ⃗xt = (xt, yt, wt, zt): U →R4 such that ⃗xt(U ∩Σ) ⊆
{(x, y, 0, 0)} ⊆R4, and
ft = h(p) −x2
t + y2
t + w2
t + z2
t .
Proof. First, consider the case that f0 and f1 agree in an open set N containing Crit(h). In this
case, we start with the 1-parameter family ft = t · f1 + (1 −t) · f0. Notice that ft has no critical
points along Σ except for those in Crit(h) since it is constant in t on Σ. A generic perturbation of
ft near the set of critical points in W \ Σ will be Morse at all but ﬁnitely many t ∈[0, 1], where a
birth-death singularity, or a critical value switch occurs. Since ft is ﬁxed on N, one does not need
to perturb near Σ.
Using the previous observation, it is suﬃcient to show that if f0, f1 ∈M(W, Σ; h) are arbitrary
Morse functions, then there is a 1-parameter family ( ˆft)t∈[0,1] such that ˆft ∈M(W, Σ; h) for all t,
and such that ˆf0 = f0 and ˆf1 agrees with f1 in a neighborhood of Crit(h).
For notational simplicity, let’s consider only the case that Crit(h) contains a single point p. By
the assumption that f0, f1 ∈M(W, Σ; h), there is an open neighborhood U, with coordinates maps
⃗x0 = (x0, y0, w0, z0) and ⃗x1 = (x1, y1, w1, z1), centered at p, such that fi = F h(p)

without loss of generality, that ⃗x0(U) ⊆R4 is convex.
Lemma 11.8 implies that after possibly

starting at ⃗x0 and ending at ⃗x1, such that h = h(p) −x2

Note that φt := ⃗x−1

Lemma 11.13. Suppose h: Σ →[0, 2] is a Morse function, all of whose critical points have index 1
and all of whose critical values are in (0, 1). If f0, f1 ∈Mnice(W, Σ; h), then there is a smooth path
ft from f0 to f1 such that ft is a nice Morse function except at ﬁnitely many t, where one of the
following occurs:
(1) Two critical points in W \ Σ exchange relative value.
(2) An index 1/2 or index 2/3 birth-death singularity occurs in W \ Σ.
Proof. Using Lemma 11.12, we can ﬁnd a generic path ft from f0 to f1, which is in M(W, Σ; h) for
all but ﬁnitely many t, where a critical value switch occurs, or a birth-death singularity occurs in
W \ Σ. We pick a path of gradient-like vector ﬁelds vt for ft, such that vt|Σ is tangent to Σ for
all t (it is straightforward to show that such vector ﬁelds exist). We note that by assumption, the
descending manifolds of Crit(f|Σ) are contained in Σ, and hence are disjoint from the ascending
manifolds of Crit(ft|W \Σ). Hence we can modify the path ft so that all critical points of any ft|W \Σ
occur in the interval (1, 2), using the “independent trajectories principle” ([Kir78, pg. 40]).
Now one can use the techniques of Cerf graphics (see [Kir78, Section 3], [Cer70], [HW73]) to
modify the path ft so that all index 1 critical points occur before the index 2 critical points, which
occur before the index 3 critical points, and so that there are also no index 0 or 4 critical points.
Generically, the path ft will be Morse and have distinct critical values for all but ﬁnitely many
t ∈[0, 1], where a critical point birth-death may occur, or a critical value switch may occur.
□
11.4. Connecting Morse functions which disagree on Σ. In this section, we describe how to
connect two Morse functions which may not agree on Σ. As a ﬁrst step, we prove a version of
[GWW12, Lemma 3.1] for link cobordisms:
Lemma 11.14. Suppose that ft : W →[0, 2] is a path of Morse functions on (W, Σ) such that

for all t, and an isotopy φt : R →R such that
ft = φt ◦f0 ◦ψ−1
t
.
Proof. If we do not require ψt to map Σ to Σ, then [GWW12, Lemma 3.1] gives us the result
immediately. We brieﬂy sketch the modiﬁcation of their proof, to the setting of link cobordisms.
First pick an isotopy φt : R →R such that ft and φt ◦f0 have the same critical values. Such a φt
exists because by assumption all critical values of ft are distinct, and there are no critical point
birth-death singularities. Write gt for φ−1

Lemma 11.15. Suppose that f ∈Mnice(W, Σ) and ht : Σ →[0, 2] is a smooth path of Morse
functions on Σ such that h0 = f|Σ and each ht has distinct critical values in the interval (0, 1).
Then there is a path ft such that each ft ∈Mnice(W, Σ; ht) and f0 = f.
Proof. The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 11.14.
Using the techniques of that lemma,
we can ﬁnd smooth isotopies φt : R →R and ψt : Σ →Σ, such that ψt is ﬁxed on ∂Σ such that
ht = φt ◦h0 ◦ψ−1
t
. Furthermore, since ht has no critical values in [1, 2], we can take φt to be the
identity on [1, 2]. One simply extends ψt to a smooth isotopy Ψt : W →W, in such a way that
Ψt is ﬁxed on ∂W, and is the identity on all of the critical points of f|W \Σ. We deﬁne the path
ft := φt ◦f0 ◦Ψ−1
t .
□
Lemma 11.16. Suppose that (W, Σ) is a link cobordism, (ht)t∈[0,1] : Σ →R is a smooth path of
Morse functions which have only index 1 critical points, and f ∈Mnice(W, Σ; h0) is a nice Morse
function. After modifying the path ht slightly near each critical value switch, there is a smooth family
of Morse functions ft such that ft ∈Mnice(W, Σ; ht), except at ﬁnitely many t, where a critical value
switch between two critical points in Crit(ht) occurs.
Proof. Since the path ht is Morse for all t, and has only index 1 critical points, we can perturb ht
slightly so that there are only ﬁnitely many times t when the critical points of ht do not have distinct
critical values. By modifying ht slightly, we can subdivide [0, 1] by picking ﬁnitely many points of
time 0 = t0 < t1 < · · · < tn = 1 such that on each interval [ti, ti+1] there is at most one critical
value switch. Note that Lemma 11.15 handled the case that there are no critical value switches of
ht on [ti, ti+1]. Hence it is suﬃcient to prove the lemma statement for each subinterval [ti, ti+1]
individually.
We now consider the claim in the case that [ti, ti+1] contains a single critical value switch. On the
interval [ti, ti+1], we will describe how to modify the path ht so that it satisﬁes a simple standard
model. Let us renormalize the interval [ti, ti−1] to be [−1, 1]. Cerf [Cer70, pg. 41] describes standard
models for critical value switches which he calls chemin descendant standard and chemin ascendant
standard. We describe a slight modiﬁcation of Cerf’s construction. Suppose ht is a path of Morse
functions on Σ which has distinct critical values except at t = 0, where two critical points, p and p′,
are involved in a critical value switch. Assume that h−1(p) > h−1(p′) while h1(p) < h1(p′). Let us
pick a bump function ˆω which is supported in a small neighborhood of p and p′ such that ˆω ≡+1
near p and ˆω ≡−1 near p′.
We now describe how to modify the path ht so that for t in a neighborhood of 0, the path ht has
a very simple local form. Fix a small ϵ > 0, and replace (ht)t∈[−1,1] with the concatenation of the
following ﬁve paths of Morse functions:


===PDF PAGE 80===
80
IAN ZEMKE
(1) The path (h−1 + tˆω)t∈[0,ϵ] from h−1 to h−1 + ϵˆω.
(2) The path (ht + ϵˆω)t∈[−1,0] from h−1 + ϵˆω to h0 + ϵˆω.
(3) The path (h0 −tϵˆω)t∈[−1,1] from h0 + ϵˆω to h0 −ϵˆω.
(4) The path (ht −ϵˆω)t∈[0,1] from h0 −ϵˆω to h−1 −ϵˆω.
(5) The path (h1 + tˆω)t∈[−ϵ,0] from h1 −ϵˆω to h1.
Note that paths (1), (2), (4) and (5) have distinct critical values, and hence Lemma 11.15 applies
to those paths. It remains to prove the claim for path (3). To extend the path (h0 −tϵˆω)t∈[−1,1] to
all of W, given an extension f−1 for t = −1 we simply extend the map ˆω to a small neighborhood
of p and p′ in W, and then use the formula ft = f−1 −(t + 1)ϵˆω. We note that except for p and
p′, the critical points of f−1 and their critical values are unchanged by this procedure. Hence if
f−1 ∈Mnice(W, Σ; h−1) then ft ∈Mnice(W, Σ; ht), except at t = 0, where a critical value switch
occurs.
□
11.5. Morse functions which respect the dividing set. Since we cannot deﬁne link Floer
homology when a link component has no basepoints, we need to consider Morse functions which
satisfy some extra compatibility requirements requirements with respect to the dividing set A.
Deﬁnition 11.17. Suppose that (W, F) is a decorated link cobordism, with F = (Σ, A). If f : W →
[0, 2] is a Morse function in Mnice(W, Σ), we say f is A-compatible if the following hold:
(1) Each component of f|−1
Σ (t) intersects A non-trivially for each t.
(2) f|A is Morse.
(3) All critical points of f and f|A have distinct values.
(4) All of the critical values of f|A are contained in the interval [0, 1].
We write Mnice
A (W, Σ) for the set of A-compatible, nice Morse functions. If h is a ﬁxed Morse function
on Σ with only index 1 critical points, we write Mnice
A (W, Σ; h) for the elements of Mnice
A (W, Σ) which
restrict to h on Σ.
Lemma 11.18. Suppose that f0, f1 ∈Mnice
A (W, Σ). Then there is a path ft : W →[0, 2] of smooth
functions which are in Mnice
A (W, Σ) except for ﬁnitely many t, where one of the following occurs:
(1) An index 1/2 or 2/3 birth-death singularity occurs between two critical points in W \Σ, with
critical values contained in the interval (1, 2).
(2) A critical value switch occurs between two critical points of f|W \Σ of the same index, or
between two critical points of f|Σ.
(3) An index 0/1 birth-death singularity of ft|A occurs along A.
(4) A critical value switch occurs between two critical points of ft|A.
(5) A critical value switch occurs between a critical point of ft|A and a critical point of ft|Σ.
(6) A critical point of ft|Σ crosses an arc of A, transversely.
Proof. First we claim that is suﬃcient to construct a path of Morse functions ht : Σ →[0, 2] between
f0|Σ and f1|Σ, with critical values in [0, 1], such that ht and ht|A have distinct critical values for all
but ﬁnitely many t, where one of the singularities (3), (4), (5) and (6) can occur, and such that each
component of h−1
t (s) intersects A non-trivially. If we have such a path, Lemma 11.15 allows us to
extend this to a path ˆft from f0 to some other Morse function ˆf1 ∈Mnice(W, Σ; f1|Σ). Lemma 11.12,
then allows us to connect ˆf1 and f1 via a path of functions which is ﬁxed on Σ, and which are nice
at all but ﬁnitely t, where one of singularities (1) and (2) may occur.
Hence, it is suﬃcient to construct a suitable path ht : Σ →[0, 2]. We can construct a path ht
which potentially has index 0/1 or index 1/2 birth-death singularities, however standard Morse
theory techniques allow us to reduce to the case that there are no index 0 or index 2 critical points
throughout (note that by assumption f0|Σ and f1|Σ have none). It follows that we can construct a
path ht which is Morse for all t. Via a generic perturbation, we can assume that all of the critical
values of ht and ht|A are all distinct, except at ﬁnitely many t where one of the singularities (3),
(4), (5) or (6) occurs. However, we still need to reason that we can pick a path ht such that each
connected component of h−1
t (s) intersects A non-trivially, for all t and s.


===PDF PAGE 81===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
81
To demonstrate that we can construct ht so that each component of h−1
t (s) intersects A non-
trivially, we argue as follows. First, pick any path ht, which has only singularities of type (3), (4),
(5) and (6). Since each ht has only index 1 critical points, each component of each h−1
t (s) represents
a non-zero class in H1(Σ; Z). Hence we can pick a collection of arcs E in Σ, with boundary on ∂Σ,
such that each component of h−1
t (s) intersects an arc in E non-trivially. We isotope the boundaries
of the curves in E so that each arc in E intersects the interior of an arc in A. Let E0 denote the
collection of arcs in int(Σ) obtained by removing a small portion of the boundary of each arc in E.
Let φt be an isotopy of the surface Σ, which is ﬁxed on ∂Σ and is supported in a neighborhood of
E0. By picking φt appropriately (e.g. viewing a neighborhood of each component of E0 as a disk,
and having φt rotate these disks), we can arrange that φt(A) intersects each component of each level
set of h0 and h1, and also that φ1(A) intersects each component of each level set of each ht. We
then consider the path h′
t deﬁned by concatenating the three paths (h0 ◦φt)t∈[0,1], (ht ◦φ1)t∈[0,1]
and (h1 ◦φ1−t)t∈[0,1].
□
11.6. Morse functions and parametrized Kirby decompositions. We now describe how a
Morse function on a decorated link cobordism gives a parametrized Kirby decomposition, well-
deﬁned up to Cerf equivalence.
Deﬁnition 11.19. Suppose that (W, F) is a decorated link cobordism with F = (Σ, A) and f : W →
[0, 2] is a Morse function with a collection of regular values b ⊆[0, 2]. Let us write b = {b0, . . . , bn+1}
with 0 = b0 < b1 < · · · < bn < bn+1 = 2. We deﬁne
Wi := f −1([bi, bi+1])
and
Σi := Σ ∩Wi.
We say that (f, b) is Kirby-type Morse data if f ∈Mnice
A (W, Σ), and on each (Wi, Σi), exactly one
of the following holds:
(1) f and f|A have no critical points.
(2) f has a single critical point, which is of index 1 or 3 and occurs in Wi \ Σi. Furthermore
f|A has no critical points.
(3) f has (arbitrarily many) critical points, all of which are index 2 and are in Wi \ Σi. Fur-
thermore f|A has no critical points.
(4) f has a single critical point, which is of index 1 and occurs in Σi. Furthermore f|A has no
critical points.
(5) f has no critical points. Furthermore f|A has a single critical point.
Furthermore, we assume only one Wi contains any index 2 critical points.
If (W, F) is a decorated link cobordism and (f, b) is a collection of Kirby-type Morse data we now
describe how to construct a parametrized Kirby decomposition
K(f, b).
In Lemma 11.20, below, we show that K(f, b) is well-deﬁned up to Cerf equivalence. Let us write
Yi = f −1(bi),
Li = Yi ∩Σ,
Ai = Σi ∩A
and
Fi = (Σi, Ai).
We note that, except for the lack of parametrization, each (Wi, Fi) satisﬁes the deﬁnition of an
elementary link cobordism (Deﬁnition 10.3). Furthermore, each (Wi, Fi) is equipped with a Morse
function fi := f|Wi, whose restriction to Σi and Ai is also Morse. To construct a parametrized Kirby
decomposition, we need to construct framed spheres Si ⊆Yi as well as parametrizing diﬀeomorphisms
Φi : W(Yi, Li, Si) →(Wi, Σi).
Suppose ﬁrst that the Morse function fi on (Wi, Σi) has no critical points. We set Si = S∅, we
pick a vector ﬁeld vi on Wi such that vi(fi) > 0 and vi|Σ is tangent to Σ. The ﬂow of vi (suitably
normalized) gives a parameterizing diﬀeomorphism
Φi : ([0, 1] × Yi, [0, 1] × Li) →(Wi, Σi).
Also note that the space of such vector ﬁelds is connected, so any two parametrizations Φi, made in
this way, are isotopic relative to {0} × Yi. The parametrized cobordism (Wi, Fi, Φi, S∅) is then an

[Pal63, Section 11] and [Juh15, Section 2.3.1].

Note that even for a ﬁxed gradient-like vector ﬁeld and coordinates near p, the construction still
relies on a choice of ϵ and λ, though the space of such λ is convex, so any two choices yield framed
spheres which are isotopic away from Li, which are related by Move (2).

can still use the above strategy to construct a parametrization Φi : W(Yi, Li, Si) →(Wi, Σi). The

will be empty, allowing us to perform the above construction whenever the choice of gradient-like
vector ﬁeld is generic (paths of gradient-like vector ﬁelds may of course result in handleslides, as we
discuss in the following lemma).

The above construction of K(f, b) several choices of auxiliary data, such as a choice of local
coordinates and gradient-like vector ﬁeld. Nevertheless, we have the following:
Lemma 11.20. If (W, F) is a decorated link cobordism and (f, b) is a collection of Kirby-type Morse
data, then any two choices of gradient-like vector ﬁelds and local coordinates yield Cerf equivalent
parametrized Kirby decompositions of (W, F).
Proof. It is suﬃcient to show the claim when |b| = 2, and (W, F) is an elementary cobordism,
except for the fact that it lacks a parametrization. We ﬁrst consider the case that (W, F) is of type
(EPC-1) or (EPC-4). We further restrict to the case that the Morse function f has at most one
critical point. Recall that the construction of K(f, b) depended on a choice of ϵ > 0, bump function
λ, local coordinates ⃗x and gradient-like vector ﬁeld v.
First, note that changing ϵ or the bump function λ only aﬀects the parametrization up to an
A-adapted isotopy or an isotopy of Si away from Li (Moves (1) and (2)).
If ⃗x = (x1, x2, x3, x4) is a choice of coordinates in Coorp(U, f), deﬁned in a neighborhood U of p, let
V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f) denote the space of vector ﬁelds v which have the prescribed form in a neighborhood
of p, with respect to ⃗x, such that v|Σ is tangent to Σ, and v(f) > 0 away from p.
The space
V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f) is convex, and hence connected. It is straightforward to also see that V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f) is
non-empty. If v1, v2 ∈V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f), we can connect them by a path in V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f), which results
in changing the parametrization by Moves (1) and (2).
Next, suppose ⃗x0 and ⃗x1 are two choices of coordinates. By examining the construction, we see
that restricting the domain of a set of coordinates has no eﬀect, provided ϵ is chosen suﬃciently
small.
Hence, we may assume that ⃗x0 and ⃗x1 are deﬁned on the same neighborhood of p.
By
Lemma 11.6, the space of local coordinates which put f into standard form has two components,
which can be related by simultaneously reversing the signs of the ﬁrst and last coordinates. Switching
signs of these coordinates results in Move (5). If ⃗x0 and ⃗x1 are homotopic via a path (⃗xt)t∈[0,1] in
Coorp(U, f), we ﬁrst pick a v0 ∈V(W, Σ, ⃗x0, f). It is straightforward to extend v0 to a path (vt)t∈[0,1]
of vector ﬁelds such that vt ∈V(W, Σ, ⃗xt, f) for all t ∈[0, 1]. Hence, changing from ⃗x0 to ⃗x1 results
in an A-admissible isotopy and an isotopy of the framed sphere away from Li (Moves (1) and (2)).
We now consider the case that (W, F) is of type (EPC-4) but f has multiple index 2 critical points.
In this case, if ⃗x is a choice of coordinates on a neighborhood of all of the critical points, then the
descending and ascending manifolds of a generic v ∈V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f) will be transverse, and hence
disjoint. For a generic path of vector ﬁelds in V(W, Σ, ⃗x, f), there will be ﬁnitely many points of
time when there is a single ﬂow line between two critical points of f. At such a point of time, a


===PDF PAGE 84===
84
IAN ZEMKE
handleslide of one of the framed 1-spheres occurs, which results in Move (9). Over the course of time
when the path of gradient-like vector ﬁelds have no ﬂow lines amongst each other, the associated
parametrized Kirby decompositions are related to each other by a sequence of Moves (1) and (2).
Finally, we note that the case when (W, F) is of type (EPC-3) (a framed 0-spheres along L) can
be handled similarly to the case of framed 0-spheres away from L, except we use Lemma 11.7 to
connect two coordinate charts deﬁned in a neighborhood of p.
□
Lemma 11.21. Given a parametrized Kirby decomposition K of (W, F), one can ﬁnd Kirby-type
Morse data (f, b) for (W, F) such that
K = K(f, b).
Proof. One can modify the construction from [Juh15, Lemma 2.14] to get a Morse function on each
elementary cobordism which induces the correct parametrization for a choice of gradient-like vector
ﬁeld. By modifying the Morse functions near the boundary, one can ensure that they glue together
to form a smooth function on all of W.
□

Using this fact, it is straightforward to see that (after perhaps a small perturbation of the path
ft) the parametrized Kirby decompositions K(fa, ba) and K(fb, bb) diﬀer by Move (10), Move (11),

A (W, Σ) except for
a single point of time, where an index 1/2 or 2/3 birth-death singularity occurs in W \ Σ, or a
critical value switch occurs between two critical points of ft of the same index. Consider ﬁrst the
case of a critical point birth-death singularity. Near the point of time ti when the singularity occurs,
by perturbing the path of functions slightly, we can assume that for a small subinterval of time
around the singularity, the path of functions ft is supported in a small ball containing the critical
points, and takes on a simple normal form. The existence of such normal forms is established in
[Cer70] (compare [Juh15, pg. 17]). As such the parametrized Kirby decompositions immediately
preceding and following the singularity may be related by Move (6) or (7). Next we consider the
case of a critical value switch of critical points of the same index. Note that since the ft are nice
Morse functions, if a critical value switch occurs, it either involves critical points which are both in
W \ Σ, or critical points which are both on Σ. As with critical point cancellations, by perturbing
the path ft slightly, we can assume that in a small interval of time around the singularity that ft
is constant outside of a neighborhood of the two critical points, and inside of the neighborhood, it
takes on a simple normal form (see the proof of Lemma 11.16). If the two critical points are in
W \ Σ, then K(fa, ba) and K(fb, bb) can be taken to be equal if the two critical points have index
2, and are otherwise related by Move (8) if the two critical points are of index 1 or 3. Similarly if
the two critical points involved in the switch are contained in Σ, then they are both of index 1, and
K(fa, ba) and K(fb, bb) are related by Move (8).
□
We make an additional remark about Move (14), where a critical point of Σ crosses A. It is
easier to visualize the move in terms of the dividing set changing, with a ﬁxed Morse function on
(W, Σ) (these two viewpoints are related by Lemma 11.14). In this case, we can view a subarc
of A (containing a critical point of ft|A) as being pushed through the critical point of ft|Σ. We
demonstrate this pictorially in Figure 11.1. See also Figure 12.4, where we prove invariance of the
move.
12. Construction and invariance of the cobordism maps
In this section, we provide a precise construction of the link cobordism maps and prove Theorem A,
invariance.
In Sections 12.1–12.3 we deﬁne the maps for elementary parametrized link cobordisms. In Sec-
tion 12.4 we deﬁne the maps in the case that each component of W and F intersects a component
of Y1 and Y2 non-trivially, and prove invariance for the maps associated to such cobordisms. In
Section 12.5, we describe how to puncture a link cobordism to introduce additional boundary com-
ponents in order to deﬁne the cobordism maps more generally. Finally, we show invariance of the
link cobordism maps under this puncturing procedure, completing the proof of Theorem A.
12.1. Maps induced by elementary parametrized link cobordisms of type (EPC-1) and
(EPC-4). We now consider an elementary parametrized link cobordism W = (W, Fσ, Φ, S): (Y1, L1) →
(Y2, L2) where S = S∅(in which case W is of type (EPC-1)), or where S is a framed 0-sphere, 1-
dimensional link or 2-sphere which does not intersect L1 (in which case W is of type (EPC-4)). In
both cases, the dividing set is a collection of arcs from L1 to L2 and the underlying surface of F is
a disjoint union of annuli.
Deﬁne a dividing set A0 ⊆[0, 1] × L1 by the formula
A0 := Φ−1(A).

We need to show the above expression is invariant under the choice of isotopic A′
0, as well as invariant
under isotopies of Φ, relative to {0} × Y1. The following deﬁnition will be useful:
Deﬁnition 12.1. Suppose that A is a dividing set on [0, 1]×L. We say that A is admissible if each
arc of A which goes from {0} × L to {1} × L is transverse to {s} × L for each s ∈[0, 1]. If At is a
1-parameter family of dividing sets on [0, 1] × L, which is ﬁxed pointwise on {0, 1} × L, we say that
At is an admissible isotopy if each At is admissible.
We state the following simple lemma about admissible isotopies, which will also be helpful later
in Section 12.2.
Lemma 12.2. Suppose A1 and A2 are two admissible dividing sets on [0, 1] × L, which are isotopic
relative to {0, 1} × L. Then A1 and A2 are admissibly isotopic relative to {0, 1} × L.
Proof. For notational simplicity, assume that L has only one component. Note that if an arc A of
A1 goes from {0} × L to {0} × L, then it bounds a bigon, which can only contain other arcs of A1
which go from {0} × L to {0} × L. Hence such arcs may be isotoped very close to {0} × L without
moving any arcs which go from {0} × L to {1} × L. Hence we may reduce to the case that no arcs
go from {0} × L to {0} × L. We can similarly reduce to the case that there are also no arcs which
go from {1} × L to {1} × L.
In the case that A1 and A2 have the same endpoints, and both only have arcs going from {0} × L
to {1}×L, it is straightforward to see that A1 and A2 are admissibly isotopic if and only if whenever
A1 and A2 are arcs in A1 and A2 (resp.) which have the same endpoints, we have
#(A1 ∩([0, 1] × {p})) = #(A2 ∩([0, 1] × {p}))
for any p ∈L which is not the endpoint of an arc on either end. The algebraic intersection number
of an arc with [0, 1] × {p} is unchanged by arbitrary isotopies which ﬁx {0, 1} × L, and hence two


===PDF PAGE 87===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
87
dividing sets are isotopic relative to {0, 1} × L if and only if they are admissibly isotopic relative to
{0, 1} × L.
□
Lemma 12.3. Isotopies of the parameterizing diﬀeomorphism Φ through diﬀeomorphisms ﬁxing
{0} × Y1 do not aﬀect FW,Fσ,Φ,S,s for type (EPC-1) and (EPC-4) elementary cobordisms.
Proof. Isotopies of Φ which ﬁx Σ pointwise have no eﬀect on the cobordism map. Similarly isotopies
of Φ which are supported only in a neighborhood of Σ, but which ﬁx {1} × L1 only aﬀect the
composition by isotoping the pullback of the dividing set, relative to {0, 1} × L1, and hence possibly
changing the map ψ∗. By Lemma 12.2, such an isotopy of the dividing set can be taken to be an
admissible isotopy, which clearly has no eﬀect (since the resulting ψ is unchanged). It remains to just
check the eﬀect of isotopies supported in a neighborhood of {1} × L1. If τ denotes a diﬀeomorphism
which twists a neighborhood of {1} × L1, then precomposing Φ with τ changes (Φ|{1}×Y1)∗to
(Φ|{1}×Y1)∗(τ∗) but also changes ψ∗to (τ −1)∗(ψ∗) (since the pullback under Φ of the dividing set
changes). Since (τ −1)∗commutes with FY1,L1,S,Φ−1(s), as τ does not move the framed sphere S, the
factors of (τ)∗and (τ −1)∗cancel and the composition is unchanged.
□

12.3. Maps induced by elementary parametrized link cobordisms of type (EPC-3). Sup-
pose that (W, Fσ, Φ, S0) is an elementary cobordism of type (EPC-3) from (Y1, L1) to (Y2, L2). Here
S0 is a framed 0-sphere in Y1, which intersects L1 along two arcs.
Recall that we constructed a surface
Σ(L1, S0) = ([0, 1] × L1) ∪B ∪([1, 2] × L1(S0)) ⊆W(Y1, S0),
which is diﬀeomorphic to a disjoint union of a pair-of-pants together with some cylinders. The subset
B is identiﬁed with [−1, 1] × [−1, 1]. Deﬁne the embedded 1-complex
L = ({1} × L) ∩({1} × L(S0)) ⊆Σ(L1, S0),
which can think of as a subset the critical level set of a Morse function on Σ(L1, S0), with a single
index 1 critical point on the band.
The parameterizing diﬀeomorphism
Φ: W(Y1, L1, S0) →(W, Fσ)
is assumed to have the property that Φ−1(A) does not intersect the band region B ⊆Σ(L1, S0).
Furthermore, the dividing set A consists entirely of arcs going from the incoming boundary to the
outgoing boundary.
We note that the dividing set Φ−1(A) could wind many times around the
annular subsets of Σ(L1, S0) identiﬁed with [0, 1] × L1 or [1, 2] × L1(S0).
In analogy with Deﬁnition 12.1, we make the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 12.5. Suppose A is a dividing set on Σ(L, S0), such that all arcs go from the incoming
boundary to the outgoing boundary. We say A is admissible if no arcs intersect B, and all arcs
intersect L transversely at a single point. If At is a 1-parameter family of dividing sets on Σ(L1, S0)
which are ﬁxed on the {0} × L and {2} × L(S0), then we say At is an admissible isotopy if At is
admissible for all t.
Lemma 12.6. Suppose that A1 and A2 are admissible dividing sets on Σ(L1, S0). If A1 and A2
are isotopic through dividing sets ﬁxed on {0} × L1 and {2} × L1(S0) and not intersecting the point
(0, 0) ∈B, then A1 and A2 are admissibly isotopic.
Proof. On any component of Σ(L1, S0) which does not intersect B, the claim is obvious, so suppose
without loss of generality that L1 is a one (resp. two) component link which is separated (resp.
joined) by the band B. Suppose for deﬁniteness, that L1 is a two component link which is joined by
B. The case that L1 is a one component link which is separated by the band follows by turning the
picture upside down.
Let p−, p+ ∈L1 denote the two center points of the framed sphere S0. Let C−and C+ be curves
on ([0, 1]×L1)∪B, each consisting of a path on [0, 1]×L1 from a point on ({0}×L1)\Ai, to (p−, 1)
or (p+, 1), respectively, concatenated with one of the arcs [−1, 0] × {0} or [0, 1] × {0}, respectively,

We have the following:
Lemma 12.7. For an elementary parametrized cobordism (W, Fσ, Φ, S0) of type (EPC-3), isotopies
of the parametrization Φ which ﬁx {0} × Y1 and do not map the point (0, 0) ∈B to A do not aﬀect
the map FW,Fσ,Φ,S0,s.
Proof. Isotopies which are ﬁxed pointwise on Σ(L1, S0) clearly have no eﬀect, so we can just consider
isotopies supported in a neighborhood of Σ(L1, S0). By the same argument as in Lemma 12.3, the
maps are independent of isotopies of Φ which are supported in a neighborhood of {2} × L1(S0).
Hence it remains to show that the maps are invariant under isotopies which are supported in a
neighborhood of Σ(L1, S0) and are ﬁxed on {0} × Y1 and {2} × Y1(S0). Suppose Φt is such an
isotopy. Since Φt is ﬁxed on {2} × Y1(S0), the map FW,F,Φt,S0,s only depends on Φt through the
dividing set At := Φ−1
t (A) on Σ(L1, S0). By Lemma 12.6, we can assume that A0 and A1 are
admissibly isotopic, i.e., the dividing sets At never pass through the band B, and the curves At
remain transverse to L throughout.

12.4. Invariance of the cobordism maps from a parametrized Kirby decomposition. If
(W, Fσ) is a link cobordism such that each component of W has a nonempty incoming and outgoing

Kirby decomposition, K, with elementary parametrized cobordisms Wi = (Wi, Fσi
i , Φi, Si), and
deﬁne the maps
FW,Fσ,s,K =
Y

,Φi,Si,s|Wi,
where the maps FWi,F

,Φi,Si,s|Wi are the maps deﬁned in the previous subsections for elementary
parametrized link cobordisms.
We now prove a slightly weaker version of Theorem A, where each component of W and F
intersects a component of Y1 and Y2 non-trivially. In Section 12.5, where we may have to puncture
the cobordism to introduce new ends, ﬁnishing the proof of Theorem A.
Theorem A′. Suppose that (W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is a decorated link cobordism and each
component of W and F intersects a component of both Y1 and Y2 non-trivially. The maps FW,Fσ,s,K
are independent of the parametrized Kirby decomposition, K, up to ﬁltered, equivariant chain homo-
topy.
Proof. By Theorem 11.5, it is suﬃcient to show that the maps FW,Fσ,s,K are equal for Cerf equivalent
parametrized Kirby decompositions. Hence it is suﬃcient to check invariance from Moves (1)–(14)
from Deﬁnition 11.4.
Move (1): We consider A-adapted isotopies (Deﬁnition 11.1). First note that if
φ: (W, Σ) →(W, Σ),
is a diﬀeomorphism such that φ|Y1 = id, then we can push forward K under φ using Equation (50)
to get a parametrized Kirby decomposition φ∗(K), which is tautologically a parametrized Kirby
decomposition of (W, φ(Fσ)). Tautologically, we have the relation
(53)
FW,(Σ,A)σ,s,K ≃(φ|−1
Y2 )∗FW,(Σ,φ(A))σ,φ∗(s),φ∗(K).
We now consider the case that φ = φ1 for an A-adapted isotopy φt : (W, Σ) →(W, Σ). Suppose ﬁrst
that φt is supported in a neighborhood of L2 (i.e. φt just twists Σ near L2). Using the deﬁnitions
of the maps for elementary parametrized cobordisms from Sections 12.1–12.3, one easily veriﬁes the
relation
FW,(Σ,φ(A))σ,s,φ∗(K) ≃(φ|Y2)∗FW,(Σ,A)σ,s,K.
Combining with Equation (53), we obtain invariance under isotopies φt which are supported in a
neighborhood of L2. Since isotopies of (Y2, L2) which are ﬁxed on L2 induce the identity map, by
naturality, we can reduce to the case that φ1|Y2 = id.
Assuming now that φ1|Y2 = id, Equation (53) reads
FW,(Σ,A)σ,s,K ≃FW,(Σ,φ(A))σ,s,φ∗(K).
Recall that our goal is to show that the left side of the above equation is FW,(Σ,A)σ,s,φ∗(K). We
note that by assumption, the 1-parameter family of dividing sets At := φ1−t(A) connects φ(A)
and A. Furthermore, since φt is A-adapted, each of the dividing sets At makes φ∗(K) into a valid


===PDF PAGE 92===
92
IAN ZEMKE
parametrized Kirby decomposition (i.e. each level of K is an elementary parametrized cobordism
with the dividing set induced by At). Hence it is suﬃcient to show invariance of the cobordism
maps FW,(Σ,A)σ,s,K under changing A through a 1-parameter family which is ﬁxed on ∂Σ and has
the property that K is a parametrized Kirby decomposition of (W, (Σ, At)σ) for all t. To establish
this fact, note that we can modify the isotopy At so that it decomposes into a sequence of isotopies,
each of which satisﬁes one of the following:
(1) At is supported in a neighborhood of L = ∂Σi ∩∂Σi+1, where Σi denotes the subset of Σ
inside of the level Wi of K.
(2) At is ﬁxed on ∂Σi, for all i.
Isotopies of the ﬁrst type are easily seen to have no eﬀect, since they change the map associated
to Wi by post-composition by a diﬀeomorphism map τ∗for twisting along L, and they change the
map for Wi+1 by pre-composition by (τ −1)∗. These two factors clearly cancel and the overall map
is unchanged. Similarly isotopies of the second kind have no eﬀect, by Lemmas 12.3, 12.4, and 12.7.

Let dS : Y (S) →Y (S′) denote the induced map.
We pull back the

Move (4): Invariance under adding or removing a trivial cylinder is essentially a tautology. The
map induced by a trivial cylinder is a diﬀeomorphism map, and all of the other maps involve pre-
or post-composition with an analogous diﬀeomorphism. It is easy to check that combining a trivial
cylinder with another elementary cobordism (from the left or the right) does not aﬀect the maps.
Move (5): We now consider invariance under changing the orientation of the framed sphere S.
We argue in the case of a framed sphere S, attached away from L. The case that S is attached
along L follows from similar considerations. View S as an embedding of Sk × D3−k into Y . Let
σ: R4 →R4 denote the map σ(x, y, w, z) = (−x, y, w, −z).
Viewing Sk × D3−k as a subset of
Rk+1 × R3−k, we deﬁne S := S ◦σ. Note that σ induces a map σY : Y (S) →Y (S), as well as
σW : W(Y, L, S) →W(Y, L, S). We have,
σY
∗FY,L,S,s = FY,L,S,σW
∗s,
which follows from the fact that the maps do not depend on a choice of orientation of the Sk factor of
the framed spheres S if k ∈{0, 1, 2}. Since the parametrizations Φ and Φ′ also diﬀer by composition
with σW , the composed maps are unchanged.
Move (6): Invariance under index 1/2 critical point birth-deaths of f|W \Σ follows from the same
triangle map computation used to prove the analogous result about the closed 3-manifold invariants
[Zem15, Theorem 9.7], which is a slight adaptation of the original argument by Ozsv´ath and Szab´o
in [OS06, Lemma 4.16], to handle our slightly diﬀerent deﬁnition of the 1-and 3-handle maps.
Move (7): Invariance under index 2/3 critical point birth-deaths of f|W \Σ follows from [Zem15,
Theorem 9.11]. Note that although generically a framed 2-sphere in Y could intersect the link at
a collection of points, by our construction, any framed 2-sphere appearing in a parametrized Kirby
decomposition does not intersect the link L, which allows us to use the same model computation as
in the case of [Zem15, Theorem 9.11]. Again note that an analogous count of holomorphic triangles
was proven by Ozsv´ath and Szab´o in [OS06, Lemma 4.17].
Move (8): Invariance under critical value switches between two index 1 or two index 3 critical
points of f|W \Σ follows from Lemma 5.4, where we showed that pairs of 1-handle maps or pairs of
3-handle maps commute with each other.
Move (9): Invariance from handleslides amongst the components of a framed 1-dimensional link
follows identically to [OS06, Lemma 4.14]. If (Σ, α′, α, β) is subordinate to an α-bouquet for S1,
and (S1)′ is obtained from S1 via a handleslide, then a triple subordinate to a bouquet for (S1)′


===PDF PAGE 94===
94
IAN ZEMKE
can be obtained by handlesliding the associated curves in α′ over each other. Invariance from such
handleslide follows using the associativity relations, and a model computation (performed in [OS06]).

satisﬁed when the basepoints arise in critical value switch of critical points of f|A where one induces
a type T + or T −map, and the other induces a type S+ or S−map.
Move (12): We now consider the eﬀect of switching the order of two index 1 critical points on
Σ. The maps associated to critical points of f along Σ are the compound 1-handle/band maps from
Section 7.3. The triangle map computation from Proposition 5.3 implies that the 1-handle maps
commute with the band maps. By Lemma 5.4 the 1-handle maps commute with each other. Thus it
remains just to show that the band maps can be commuted amongst themselves. This now follows
from Proposition 9.13. Note that if both band maps are z-band maps or both are w-band maps,
then the maps always commute, though if one band map is w-band while the other is a z-band, we
must be careful about the conﬁgurations of the ends of the of the bands, because Proposition 9.13
has some non-trivial requirements on conﬁguration of the ends of the bands. We note that the
hypotheses of Proposition 9.13 are always satisﬁed when the two bands are induced from a surface
with divides with a Morse function f such that f has two index one critical points, and f|A has no
critical points.
Move (13): We now consider a critical value switch between critical points of f|Σ and f|A. A
critical point of f|Σ induces compound 1-handle/band map, and a critical point of f|A induces a
quasi-stabilization map. The 1-handle maps commute with quasi-stabilization maps by Lemma 8.1
and the band maps by the triangle map computation of Proposition 5.3. Thus it is suﬃcient to
analyze when the band maps commute with quasi-stabilization maps.
Commutation of the quasi-stabilization maps S◦

12.5. Constructing the cobordism maps in the presence of empty ends. In this section we
construct cobordism maps when a link cobordism does not have enough incoming or outgoing ends.
To deﬁne the maps for such link cobordisms, we construct the cobordism maps by puncturing the
link cobordism (i.e. removing a 4-ball) at a collection of points along the dividing set, and then pre-
and post-composing with the 0-handle and 4-handle maps from Section 5.2.

13. The composition law
In this Section we prove the composition law:
Theorem B. Suppose that (W, Fσ): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is decorated link cobordism which decom-
poses as the composition
(W, Fσ) = (W2, Fσ2
2 ) ◦(W1, Fσ1

If s1 and s2 are Spinc structures on W1 and W2 respectively, then
FW2,Fσ2

Proof. Suppose ﬁrst that each component of Wi and Fi intersects an incoming and outgoing end
of the cobordism (Wi, Fσ
i ) non-trivially. Take parametrized Kirby decompositions K1 = K(f1, b1)
of (W1, F1) and K2 = K(f2, b2) of (W2, F2), induced by very nice Morse functions f1 and f2 with
collections of regular values b1 and b2. Also pick gradient-like vector ﬁelds v1 and v2, inducing the
decompositions. We can assume that f1 and f2 patch together, and v1 and v2 patch together to
give Morse functions and gradient-like vector ﬁelds on (W, F). The collections of regular values b1
and b2 induce a collection b for f. This yields a decomposition, K(f, b), of (W, F) into elementary
parametrized cobordisms, which is the one obtained by just stacking K1 and K2. We note that
K(f, b) may not be a parametrized Kirby decomposition since the terms are in the wrong order,
and there may be two 2-handle cobordisms. Nonetheless, the composition of the cobordism maps
for each elementary parametrized cobordism in the decomposition is FW2,Fσ2
2
,s2FW1,Fσ1

,s1.
Notice that the index 1, 2 and 3 dimensional critical points of f1 which are away from Σ1 have
unstable manifolds which do not intersect Σ, and similarly the critical points of f2 away from Σ have
stable manifolds which do not intersect Σ. Hence, assuming that (f, v) is generic, we can modify the
Morse functions to pull all of the critical points of f1|W1\Σ1 above any critical point of f2|Σ2 along
Σ, and then pull the index 3 critical points of f1 above the index 1 and 2 critical points of f2, and
pull the index 2 critical points of f1 above the index 1 critical points of f1.
The composition of the map for each piece of the stacked decomposition is invariant under pulling
the 3-handles of f1 above the 1-handles of f2 by Lemma 5.4. The composition is also invariant of
pulling the 3-handles of f1 past the 2-handles of f2 by the triangle map computation of Proposi-
tion 5.3.
Hence it remains to show that the composition is unchanged when we pull the 1-,2- and 3-handles
of f1|W1\Σ1 past the elementary cobordisms associated to critical points of f2|Σ and f2|A.
The
critical points of f2|A induce maps which are a composition of the basepoint moving maps along
the link components, and the quasi-stabilization maps. The maps for surgery on framed spheres
away from the link commute with the basepoint moving maps by diﬀeomorphism invariance of the
maps. The 1- and 3-handles of f1 can be pulled past the critical points of f2|A without changing
the composition of the maps by Lemma 8.1 (showing that the 1-handle and 3-handle maps commute
with quasi-stabilization). The 1- and 3-handle maps can be commuted past critical points of f2|Σ
using Lemma 5.4 (to commute a 1- or 3-handle past the 1-handle map from the critical point along
Σ2) as well as the triangle map computation from Proposition 5.3 (to commute a 1- or 3-handle past
the band map for the critical point along Σ2). The 2-handles of f1 can be commuted past the critical
points of f2|A by Lemma 8.2. Similarly the 2-handles of f1 can be commuted past the critical points
of f2|Σ by Lemma 8.4 (to commute the 2-handle maps past the band maps), and Proposition 5.3
(to commute the 2-handle maps past the 1-handle map).

98
IAN ZEMKE
Hence FW2,Fσ2

,s2FW1,Fσ1

,s1 is equal to the composition of maps induced by a decomposition of
(W, F) into parametrized elementary cobordisms, which has elementary cobordisms satisfying the
requirements of a parametrized Kirby decomposition, except that there are exactly two adjacent
terms corresponding to surgery on framed 1-dimensional links. As W is obtained from the union of
the two 2-handle pieces by adding 1-handles and 3-handles, a Spinc structure deﬁned on the union of
the two 2-handle cobordisms extends uniquely to a Spinc structure on all of W. Hence Lemma 5.10
(the composition law for the 2-handle maps) the theorem in this case.
Finally, we need to consider the case not all of the components of Wi or Fi intersect both an
incoming and outgoing ends of Wi. The cobordism maps, in this case, are deﬁned using Equation (55)
by removing a collection of 4-balls which intersect Fi standardly. We note that the 4-handle maps for
(W1, F1) can always be commuted past each of the elementary cobordism maps in the composition for
(W2, F2). Similarly all of the 0-handle maps can be commuted past all of the elementary cobordism
maps in the composition for (W1, F1). Using the version of the composition law for link cobordisms
(Wi, Fi) where each component of Wi and Fi intersects both an incoming and outgoing boundary
component of Wi non-trivially, we obtain the general version of Theorem B.
□
14. Algebraic reduction to the graph TQFT
In [Zem15], the author constructed a graph TQFT for Heegaard Floer homology. In this section,
we relate the reductions of the link cobordism maps from this paper to the graph TQFT maps.
Throughout this section, we restrict to surfaces with divides which are colored using exactly two
colors, one for the w-basepoints and regions, and one for the z-basepoints and regions. We will
write U for the variable corresponding to the w-basepoints and regions, and we will write V for
the variable corresponding to the z-basepoints and regions. It is straightforward to generalize the
results of this section to more general colorings, as long as no w-basepoint or region shares the same
color as a z-basepoint or region.
If (Y, L) is a 3-manifold with a multi-based link, we note that
CFL−(Y, L, s) ⊗F2[U,V ] F2[U, V ]/(V −1) ∼= CF −(Y, w, s)
and
CFL−(Y, L, s) ⊗F2[U,V ] F2[U, V ]/(U −1) ∼= CF −(Y, z, s −PD[L]).
The change in Spinc structure of the U = 1 reduction is a consequence of Lemma 3.3.
The link cobordism maps FW,F,s thus naturally induce two maps on CF −, which we call the
algebraic reductions of FW,F,s. We will write FW,F,s|U=1 or FW,F,s|V =1, for these reductions.
We now state the main results of this section (note that the precise deﬁnitions of ribbon-equivalence
and ribbon 1-skeletons will be provided in Section 14.1):
Theorem C. Suppose that (W, F): (Y1, L1) →(Y2, L2) is a decorated link cobordism with F =
(Σ, A). If Γ(Σw) and Γ(Σz) are choices of ribbon 1-skeletons of Σw and Σz, then
FW,F,s|V =1 ≃F B
W,Γ(Σw),s
and
FW,F,s|U=1 ≃F A
W,Γ(Σz),s−PD[Σ].
See Deﬁnition 14.5 for the precise deﬁnition of a ribbon 1-skeleton.
The following two results are immediate corollaries of the above theorem:
Corollary D. If (W, Γ) and (W, Γ′) are ribbon-equivalent ribbon graph cobordisms (Deﬁnition 14.4),
then
F A
W,Γ,s ≃F A
W,Γ′,s
and
F B
W,Γ,s ≃F B
W,Γ′,s.
Corollary E. The V = 1 reduction of FW,F,s,
FW,F,s|V =1 : CF −(Y1, w1, s|Y1) →CF −(Y2, w2, s|Y2),
depends only on the ribbon surface Σw and the Spinc structure s. The U = 1 reduction of FW,F,s,
FW,F,s|U=1 : CF −(Y1, z1, s −PD[L1]) →CF −(Y2, z2, s −PD[L2]),
depends only on the ribbon surface Σz, and the Spinc structure s −PD[Σ].
