14.1. Deﬁnitions and topological preliminaries. In this section, we provide the topological
deﬁnitions and some preliminary results used in the statement of Theorem C and the subsequent
corollaries.
Deﬁnition 14.1. A ribbon graph cobordism between two multi-based 3-manifolds (Y1, w1) and
(Y2, w2) is a pair (W, Γ) such that
(1) W is a cobordism from Y1 to Y2.
(2) Γ is an embedded graph in W such that Γ ∩∂W = w1 ∪w2 and each basepoint in w1 ∪w2
has valence 1 in Γ.
(3) Each vertex of Γ has valance at least 1.
(4) Γ is decorated with a choice of cyclic ordering of the edges adjacent to each vertex.
The above deﬁnition naturally inspires the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 14.2. A ribbon surface cobordism between two multi-based 3-manifolds (Y1, w1) and
(Y2, w2) is a pair (W, R) such that W is a cobordism from Y1 to Y2, and R ⊆W is an oriented
surface with boundary and corners such that the following holds:
(1) R ∩Yi is a ﬁnite collection of closed intervals, each containing exactly one of the basepoints
in wi. Furthermore, each basepoint in wi is contained in a component of R ∩Yi.
(2) The corners of R correspond exactly to the boundaries of the closed intervals forming R∩Yi.
(3) R contains no closed components.
Lemma 14.3. Given a ribbon graph cobordism (W, Γ): (Y1, w1) →(Y2, w2), there is an induced
ribbon surface RΓ ⊆W, which is well-deﬁned up to 1-parameter families of ribbon surfaces (RΓ)t in
W, each making (W, (RΓ)t) into a ribbon surface cobordism.
Proof. We ﬁrst show how to construct the surface RΓ. Pick an orientation of each edge of Γ, and
also pick an oriented 2-plane in W at each interior vertex of Γ. Isotope the edges of Γ inside of W,
while ﬁxing the vertices, so that the edges meet in the chosen 2-plane, and such that the ordering
of the edges in the chosen oriented 2-plane ﬁeld agrees with the cyclic ordering from the ribbon
structure on Γ. Call this isotoped graph eΓ. To each vertex in eΓ ∩int W, we ﬁx an oriented disk
centered at the vertex, whose oriented tangent space agrees with the chosen oriented 2-plane ﬁeld.
Along each edge, we pick a nonzero vector ﬁeld we which is tangent to e, and positively oriented
with respect to the orientation of e. We also pick a non-vanishing vector ﬁeld ve along e, which is
orthogonal to e. We require that the pair (ve, we) restricts to an oriented basis on each of the disks
which we centered at the vertices. At the vertices of Γ ∩∂W, we have no requirement on we.
We can then build the surface RΓ by attaching strips to the disks, along the edges of eΓ, such that
(ve, we) gives an oriented basis of the tangent space along each edge.
To show uniqueness up to isotopy of the surface RΓ, we note that the construction involved
several steps: choosing an orientation of each edge, choosing an oriented 2-plane ﬁeld at each vertex,
choosing an isotopic eΓ whose edges meet in the chosen 2-plane ﬁelds, and choosing the vector ﬁelds
we. Firstly, the construction is clearly independent of the orientations of the edges of Γ. Having
ﬁxed ve, the space of non-vanishing vector ﬁelds we satisfying the stated assumptions is homotopy
equivalent to the space of maps from (e, ∂e) to (S2, {pt}), and is hence connected. Lastly, we show
independence of the choice of isotoped graph eΓ. Suppose eΓ and eΓ′ are two graphs, constructed
by isotoping Γ so that the edges meet in the chosen 2-plane ﬁelds at the vertices.
Clearly the
corresponding edges are homotopic relative to the vertices. Since W is a 4-manifold, a homotopy of
a 1-manifold can be taken to be an isotopy, so eΓ and eΓ′ are isotopic through graphs which meet in
the chosen 2-plane ﬁeld with the correct cyclic ordering at the vertices of Γ.
□
Deﬁnition 14.4. We say that two graph cobordisms (W, Γ1), (W, Γ2): (Y1, w1) →(Y2, w2) are
ribbon-equivalent if the two ribbon surfaces RΓ1 and RΓ2 are isotopic in W through ribbon surfaces.
Given a ribbon surface cobordism (W, R), we can go in the other direction, and construct a ribbon
graph cobordism (W, ΓR):


===PDF PAGE 100===
100
IAN ZEMKE
Deﬁnition 14.5. If (W, R) is a ribbon surface cobordism from (Y1, w1) to (Y2, w2), we say that a
ribbon graph Γ ⊆R is a ribbon 1-skeleton of R if the cyclic orders of Γ correspond to the orientation
of R, and if the surface RΓ (constructed in Lemma 14.3) can be constructed to lie in R, and such
that the inclusion RΓ ,→R is isotopic to a diﬀeomorphism between RΓ and R.
Note that there are many non-isotopic ribbon 1-skeletons for a ﬁxed ribbon surface (in fact there
are many which are not even isomorphic as graphs). We will not endeavor to create a set of moves
between two ribbon 1-skeletons of a ribbon surface, though presumably a small set of moves could
be established. In Figure 14.1 we show a few examples of ribbon 1-skeletons of surfaces.
Figure 14.1. Examples of diﬀerent ribbon 1-skeletons for ribbon surfaces.
The ribbon 1-skeletons are, by deﬁnition, ribbon-equivalent. The pairs of ribbon
graphs are not isomorphic as ribbon graphs. The top pair of ribbon 1-skeletons are
related by adding trivial strands. The middle pair are related by sliding an edge
across a vertex, in a way which is compatible with the cyclic orderings. The bottom
pair can be related by a sequence of such moves. Note that the bottom graphs are
isomorphic as graphs, but not as ribbon graphs.
14.2. Construction and basic relations of the graph TQFT. In this section, we recall the
construction of the graph cobordism maps from [Zem15], and prove some basic results, which will
be helpful for our purposes.
Analogous to the link cobordism maps deﬁned in this paper, the graph cobordism maps are
constructed by decomposing a graph cobordism into elementary pieces, and deﬁning a map for each
piece. There are 1-handle, 2-handle and 3-handle maps, modeled on the maps deﬁned by Ozsv´ath
and Szab´o [OS06], and similar to the ones we deﬁned for link cobordisms earlier in this paper. There
are also 0-handle and 4-handle maps, corresponding to adding or removing a copy of (S3, w), similar
to the maps deﬁned in Section 5.2. A key component of the construction of the graph cobordism
maps is a maps for graphs embedded in a ﬁxed 3-manifold. We make the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 14.6. A ribbon ﬂow-graph G = (Γ, V1, V2) in Y 3 is a tuple consisting of a ribbon graph
Γ ⊆Y , with two disjoint subset of vertices V1 and V2 in V (Γ) such that the following hold:
(1) V1 and V2 both intersect each component of Y non-trivially.
(2) Each vertex in V1 and V2 has valence 1 in Γ.


===PDF PAGE 101===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
101
(3) Each vertex in Γ has valence at least 1.
If G = (Γ, V1, V2) and G′ = (Γ′, V2, V3) are two ﬂow-graphs with Γ ∩Γ′ = V2, then the composition
of G′ and G is deﬁned by concatenation: G′ ◦G = (Γ′ ∪Γ, V1, V3).
In [Zem15], a graph action map was deﬁned deﬁned. If G = (Γ, V1, V2) is a ribbon ﬂow-graph in a
ﬁxed 3-manifold Y , the graph action map is a chain map
AG,s : CF −(Y, V1, s) →CF −(Y, V2, s).
There is a natural variation, BG,s, with the same domain and range, which will discuss later.
If G = (Γ, V1, V2) is a ﬂow-graph in Y , then we can construct a graph cobordism ([0, 1] ×
Y, ˆΓ): (Y, V1) →(Y, V2), such that the projection of ˆΓ onto Y is the graph Γ. The graph cobordism
maps F A
[0,1]×Y,ˆΓ,s and F B
[0,1]×Y,ˆΓ,s are equal to the graph action maps AG,s and BG,s, respectively.
There are two key constituent maps which feature in the construction of the graph action map:
the relative homology maps, and the free-stabilization maps.
We now describe the relative homology maps. Given a path λ between two basepoints w1 and w2
in Y , there is a −1 graded endomorphism

which satisﬁes
∂Aλ + Aλ∂= Uw1 + Uw2.

in the Heegaard surface, and then deﬁning

14.3. Constructing the graph action map. To deﬁne the graph action map AG,s, one subdivides
the graph Γ by adding extra vertices, and writes G (now subdivided) as a composition of elementary
ﬂow-graphs. Such a decomposition is termed a Cerf decomposition of the graph. By deﬁnition, there
are three types of elementary ﬂow-graphs G = (Γ, V1, V2):
(EFG-1): V (Γ) = V1 ⊔V2 and all edges go from V1 to V2;
(EFG-2): V (Γ) = V1 ⊔V2 ⊔{v0} and all edges either connect a vertex in V1 to V2 or connect a
vertex in V1 ∪V2 to {v0};
(EFG-3): V (Γ) = V1 ⊔V2 and there is exactly one edge which connects two vertices in V1 or two
vertices in V2.


===PDF PAGE 103===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
103
Examples of the three subtypes of elementary ribbon ﬂow-graphs are shown in Figure 14.2. Note
than any ﬂow-graph can be subdivided so that it is a concatenation of elementary ﬂow-graphs. In
[Zem15, Theorem 7.6] it is shown that the graph action map is invariant under the choice of de-
composition into elementary ﬂow-graphs. Furthermore, adapting the proof of [Zem15, Theorem 7.6]
shows that the graph action map is also invariant under subdivision.

Proposition 14.7. If G = (Γ, V1, V2) is a ribbon ﬂow-graph, and G = (Γ, V1, V2) denotes the ribbon

[HMZ18, Lemma 5.9]. For general graphs, one can use Lemma 14.9, below, to reduce to the case of
trivalent graphs.

Figure 14.5. A portion of the Heegaard diagram containing the subarc
λ of L. The arc λ is the concatenation of λ1 and λ2.
Using Condition (5) of Deﬁnition 3.1 (the deﬁnition of a Heegaard diagram for a link), we can
assume that λ is immersed in the Heegaard diagram, and furthermore, we can assume that λ1 does
not intersect any of the α-curves, and that λ2 does not intersect any of the β-curves. Hence the
quantity a(λ, φ) is equal to the total change of the multiplicities of φ along λ2, i.e.,
a(λ, φ) = nw2(φ) −nz(φ).
Multiplying a(λ, φ) by # c
M(φ)U nw(φ)
w
· y and summing over all index 1 classes φ, we arrive at the
stated relation
Aλ(x) = Uw2Φw2(x) + Ψz(x)|V =1.
Analogously, by adapting the same line of reasoning yields for the map Bλ, which uses the weights
b(λ, φ) instead of a(λ, φ), we arrive at the relation
Bλ(x) = Uw1Φw1(x) + Ψz(x)|V =1,
completing the proof.
□
Analogously, we can compute the reduction of the maps Φw, after setting U = 1.
Lemma 14.13. If w ∈w is a basepoint of L = (L, w, z), let λ denote the component of L \ z which
contains w. Suppose that λ, oriented as a subset of L, goes from z1 to z2. Then
Φw|U=1 ≃Aλ + Vz1Ψz1 ≃Bλ + Vz2Ψz2.
Proof. The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma 14.12.
□
14.5. Proof of Theorem C. We can now prove our stated theorem about the algebraic reductions
of FW,F,s.
Proof of Theorem C. Let (W, F) be a decorated link cobordism with F = (Σ, A). We ﬁrst consider
the V = 1 reduction of the link cobordism maps. First note that the composition law holds for both
the graph cobordism maps, and the link cobordism maps. Noting also that the reduction formula is
obviously true for the 0-handle and 4-handle cobordism maps, we can assume that each component
of Σ intersects a component of the incoming boundary and the outgoing boundary non-trivially.
Given a ribbon 1-skeleton Γ(Σw) of Σw, we note that by using Lemma 14.9, we can reduce to
the case that Γ(Σw) has no vertices of valence more than three. Using our assumption that each
component of Σ intersects a component of the incoming and outgoing boundaries of W, we can
decompose (W, F) into a composition (Wn, Fn) ◦· · · ◦(W1, F1) where each (Wi, Fi) satisﬁes one of
the following:


===PDF PAGE 108===
108
IAN ZEMKE
(RG-1): (Wi, Fi) is diﬀeomorphic to the trace of an isotopy φt of a link in a 3-manifold, and
Γ(Σw)∩Wi consists of a collection of paths from the incoming boundary to the outgoing
boundary.
(RG-2): (Wi, Fi) is a diﬀeomorphic to the handle attachment cobordism formed by attaching
a 4-dimensional handle or collection of handles along a framed 0-sphere, 2-sphere or
collection of framed 1-spheres, in the complement of the link. Furthermore, the graph
Γ(Σw)∩Fi consists of a collection of paths from the incoming boundary to the outgoing
boundary.
(RG-3): (Wi, Fi) is diﬀeomorphic to a cobordism obtained by attaching a type-z band to the
link. Furthermore Γ(Σw) consists of a collection of paths from the incoming boundary
to the outgoing boundary.
(RG-4): (Wi, Σi) is diﬀeomorphic (as an undecorated link cobordism) to a cylindrical link cobor-
dism. The graph Γ(Σw) ∩Wi is an elementary ﬂow-graph, satisfying one of (EFG-1),
(EFG-2) or (EFG-3). The subsurface Σw ∩Wi is a regular neighborhood of the graph
Γ(Σw) ∩Wi.
We now show the claim for a link cobordism satisfying condition (RG-1).
In this case, the
induced link cobordism is the diﬀeomorphism map induced by the isotopy φ1.
It is clear that
the reduction of the diﬀeomorphism map on link Floer homology is equal to the map induced by
the same diﬀeomorphism on Heegaard Floer homology.
We now consider the claim for link cobordisms satisfying condition (RG-2). In this case, the
link cobordism maps are deﬁned by using the 4-dimensional handle attachment maps.
The 1-
handle and 3-handle maps on link Floer homology obviously reduce to the 1-handle and 3-handle
maps used to construct the graph cobordism maps. The 2-handle maps on link Floer homology
count holomorphic triangles representing homology classes of triangles with sw(ψ) = s. The graph
cobordism maps for Γ(Σw) count the same triangles.
Hence the V = 1 reduction of the link
cobordism map FWi,Fi,s|Wi coincides with F B
Wi,Γ(Σw)∩Wi,s|Wi, if (Wi, Fi) satisﬁes condition (RG-2).
Note that the U = 1 reduction of the 2-handle maps is not covered by the above argument (as we
discuss below).
We now consider the claim for link cobordisms satisfying condition (RG-3). In this case, the link
cobordism map is a type-z band map. The band map is deﬁned by counting holomorphic triangles
via the formula
F z
B(x) = Fα′,α,β(Θw
α′,α, x),
where Θw
α′,α denotes the top grw-degree generator. After setting V = 1, the generator Θw
α′,α reduces
to the top degree generator Θ+
α′,α ∈HF −(Σ, α′, α, w, s0), and hence F z
B reduces to the change of
diagrams map associated to moving the α curves, which induces the identity morphism on the level
of transitive systems of chain complexes. On the other hand, the graph cobordism ([0, 1]×Y, Γ(Σw))
is the identity graph cobordism, so the induced graph cobordism map is also the identity map.
We now consider link cobordisms satisfying condition (RG-4). Given a cobordism (Wi, Fi) satis-
fying condition (RG-4), we can subdivide the cobordism and add trivial strands to the graph, and
replace (Wi, Fi) with a sequence of cobordisms satisfying the following:
(RG-5): (Wi, Fi) is equivalent (as an undecorated link cobordism) to a cylindrical link cobordism.
The subsurface Σw ∩Wi is a regular neighborhood of Γ(Σw) ∩Wi. The graph Γ(Σw) ∩Wi
satisﬁes one of the following:
(RG-5a): Γ(Σw) is an elementary ﬂow-graph, of type (EFG-2), and the vertex v0 in the
deﬁnition of such an elementary ﬂow-graph, has valence 1.
(RG-5b): Γ(Σw) is the H-shaped graph from Figure 14.4.
The process of manipulating and decomposing link cobordisms (Wi, Fi) satisfying condition (RG-
4) into a sequence of cobordisms satisfying condition (RG-5) is illustrated in Figure 14.6.
Thus, to show that the V = 1 reduction of the link cobordism maps agree with the graph cobordism
maps for a ribbon 1-skeleton of Σw, it is suﬃcient instead to show the claim for link cobordisms
with embedded ribbon 1-skeletons satisfying one of conditions (RG-5a) or (RG-5b).

where z is the basepoint corresponding to either of the two bigons of type Σz. By Lemma 14.12, the

Ψz|V =1 ≃Bλ + Uw1Φw1.

Having proven the claim for link cobordisms satisfying one (RG-1)–(RG-4), it now follows from
the composition law that FW,F,s|V =1 ≃F B
W,Γ(Σw),s for a general decorated link cobordism (W, F).
We now consider the U = 1 reduction of the link cobordism maps. The above strategy is easily
adapted to consider these maps, however there are several slight diﬀerences. Firstly, the 2-handle
maps for FW,F,s count holomorphic triangles with underlying homology class ψ satisfying sw(ψ) = s,
while the 2-handle maps for F B
W,Γ(Σz),s count holomorphic triangles which satisfy sz(ψ) = s. However,
by [Zem17b, Lemma 3.9], one has
sw(ψ) −sz(ψ) = PD[Σ],
where [Σ] ∈H2(W, ∂W; Z) denotes the homology class of the surface in the link cobordism. Hence,
for a 2-handle cobordism, one has
FWi,Fi,s|Wi|U=1 ≃F A
Wi,Γ(Σz)∩Wi,(s−PD[Σ])|Wi.
Finally for link cobordisms satisfying (RG-5b) (but now with Σz having an H-shaped component,
instead of Σw), we note that the induced link cobordism map is Φw, for a w basepoint in the
boundary of one of the bigon components of Σw. By Lemma 14.13, we have
Φw|U=1 ≃Aλ + Vz1Ψz1,
which is the type-A graph cobordism map F A
Wi,Γ(Σz)∩Wi,(s−PD[Σ])|Wi by Lemma 14.11 (note that
the change in Spinc structures, in this case, is simply due to the change in Spinc structures of the
reductions of the link Floer complexes, from Lemma 3.3). Hence we conclude that, in general
FW,F,s|U=1 ≃F A
W,Γ(Σz),s−PD[Σ].
□
It is clear from the previous argument that the FW,F,s|V =1 reduction depends only on the graph
Γ(Σw) and s. Similarly it is clear that the reduction FW,F,s|U=1 depends only on Γ(Σz) and s −
PD[Σ]. Hence Corollary E follows.
14.6. Constructing the graph TQFT as the reduction of the link Floer TQFT. In the
previous section, we showed that the link cobordism maps reduce to the graph cobordism maps,
which were constructed using other techniques in [Zem15]. In this section, we show that the link
Floer TQFT can be used directly to construct a TQFT for cobordisms with embedded ribbon
graphs. To see this, we show that given a ribbon graph cobordism (W, Γ) one can always associate
a decorated link cobordism F(Γ), which is well-deﬁned up to a small ambiguity. We will show that
the ambiguity does not eﬀect the maps. It will then follow that we get an alternate construction
and proof of invariance of the graph cobordism maps from [Zem15].
By Lemma 14.3, to a ribbon graph cobordism (W, Γ), we can associate a ribbon surface cobordism
(W, RΓ), which is well-deﬁned up to isotopy. The normal bundle to the surface RΓ is an oriented
2-plane bundle, over a space homotopy equivalent to a graph, so it is trivializable. We can pick a
section v of the normal bundle, and use it to push oﬀa copy R′
Γ of RΓ (in such a way which keeps
∂RΓ ∩int W ﬁxed). We deﬁne a decorated link cobordism F(Γ) to be the union of RΓ and R′
Γ
(giving R′
Γ the opposite orientation). By Theorem C, we have that
FW,F(Γ),s|V =1 ≃F A
W,Γ,s.
The surface F(Γ) is not quite well-deﬁned, since the vector v is not uniquely speciﬁed, even up
to isotopy, since a choice of such a vector ﬁeld v is equivalent to a choice of map from Γ to S1. The
ambiguity thus lies in twisting along the edges of the graph. However the induced link cobordism


===PDF PAGE 111===
LINK COBORDISMS AND FUNCTORIALITY IN LINK FLOER HOMOLOGY
111
map for diﬀerent choices of the vector ﬁeld v can diﬀer by the diﬀeomorphism map induced by
twisting along doubly based unknot, but the map induced by such a twist is clearly the identity map
(one simply considers a diagram where the two basepoints of the knot are right next to each other).
Hence the map FW,F(Γ),s is independent of the choice of vector ﬁeld v, normal to RΓ.
